# Paper Set Evidence Digest

Number of papers in this paper set: 300

Each item below is a compact paper-level analysis digest. Use only this digest.

- source: Cressman, R., Song, J. W., Zhang, B. Y., & Tao, Y. (2012). Cooperation and evolutionary dynamics in the public goods game with institutional incentives. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that institutional incentives (reward, punishment, or both) can fundamentally alter the Nash equilibrium and evolutionary outcomes of the public goods game. Without incentives, the only stable outcome is full defection (zero contribution, zero efficiency). With sufficient institutional reward, partial cooperation emerges and group efficiency increases, but full cooperation is not stable. With sufficient punishment, full cooperation can be maintained, but it cannot invade a population of defectors. The combination of reward and punishment leads to full cooperation and maximal efficiency as the unique globally stable outcome. Theoretical results are supported by simulations of replicator dynamics, showing that the mean contribution (and thus efficiency) increases with the strength of incentives, and that the combined scheme is most robust.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling institutional punishment (and/or reward) in a standard public goods game increases group efficiency relative to the no-incentive baseline. The effect size and stability depend on the magnitude of the incentive, the group size, and the specific incentive scheme. For the canonical parameter set (n=4, r=1.6, E=20, A=20), institutional punishment alone can maintain full cooperation if established, but cannot invade defection; reward alone increases cooperation but not to full efficiency; the combination achieves full efficiency. The results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with similar design dimensions, especially for continuous contribution, no chat, known group size, and institutional (not peer) incentives. The findings are less directly applicable to repeated games or peer-sanctioning settings.

- source: Dong, Y. L., Sasaki, T., & Zhang, B. Y. (2019). The competitive advantage of institutional reward. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with institutional incentives, institutional reward is generally more efficient (i.e., leads to higher group average fitness) than institutional punishment, especially when players are boundedly rational and make decision errors. Without errors, a mixture of reward and punishment can be optimal for intermediate incentive sizes, but with intermediate or large decision errors, punishment almost always reduces group welfare, and reward is the most efficient. These results are robust across a range of game parameters and model variants.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that institutional punishment tends to reduce efficiency compared to reward, especially when decision errors are present. If the control (no-punishment) efficiency is already low, adding institutional punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency and may worsen it, unless the system is nearly error-free and incentives are finely tuned. The findings suggest that institutional reward is a safer and more robust mechanism for improving efficiency than punishment.

- source: Jiao, Y. H., Chen, T., & Chen, Q. (2020). Probabilistic punishment and reward under rule of trust-based decision-making in continuous public goods game. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that introducing probabilistic punishment and reward (i.e., punish/reward with probability rho < 1) into a continuous PGG can maintain high levels of cooperation and mean payoff (efficiency), even when the cost of punishment/reward is high. Traditional (always executed) punishment/reward leads to collapse of cooperation and low efficiency when costs are high. There is an optimal execution probability (rho ≈ 0.6) and moderate fine/bonus size (f = r = 1) that maximize mean payoff and cooperation, saving about 40% of the cost compared to always executing punishment/reward. If either the execution probability or the fine/bonus is too low, cooperation and efficiency remain low. The model shows that probabilistic execution is robust to high costs and can avoid the collapse of cooperation seen in traditional models.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in continuous PGGs on structured populations, enabling punishment (and reward) with a moderate probability (rather than always) can increase group efficiency compared to both no punishment and always-on punishment, especially when costs are high. The effect is robust across a range of synergy factors (mpcr), costs, and fine/bonus sizes. For prediction, the presence of probabilistic punishment (with moderate execution probability and moderate cost/fine) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to the control (no punishment) and to traditional always-on punishment, particularly when punishment/reward costs are nontrivial. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially the presence and cost of punishment, the effectiveness of punishment, and whether execution is probabilistic or deterministic.

- source: Eldakar, O. T., Farrell, D. L., & Wilson, D. S. (2007). Selfish punishment: Altruism can be maintained by competition among cheaters. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with punishment, both altruism (cooperation) and punishment can be maintained at moderate to high levels, resulting in group productivity well above the baseline of no cooperation. Punishment is effective at deterring cheating and thus increases group efficiency, but the costliness of punishment, group size, and the number of rounds all moderate this effect. When punishment is too costly or the game is too short, neither altruism nor punishment is maintained. The model also shows that punishment need not be performed by altruists: 'selfish punishers' (cheaters who punish other cheaters) can maintain high group efficiency by removing the worst cheaters, thereby protecting a pool of cooperators to exploit. The model directly emulates the structure of experimental public goods games and provides parameter sweeps for punishment cost, group size, and round length, showing their effects on equilibrium levels of cooperation and punishment, and thus on group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment in a public goods game increases group efficiency (total payoff relative to full cooperation), except when punishment is extremely costly or the game is one-shot. The model is directly parameterized in terms of group size (player_count), number of rounds (num_rounds), punishment cost (punishment_cost), and other key design dimensions. The findings suggest that the positive effect of punishment on efficiency is robust across a range of parameter values, but is diminished as punishment becomes more costly, group size increases, or the number of rounds decreases. The model does not include communication (chat), rewards, or identification of punishers, so its predictions are most applicable to games without these features. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment will increase efficiency, especially in small groups, with moderate punishment costs, and multiple rounds.

- source: Gintis, H. (2000). Strong reciprocity and human sociality. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that strong reciprocity—cooperation combined with costly punishment of defectors—can sustain high levels of cooperation and group efficiency in public goods games, even when repeated interaction is unlikely. Theoretical results show that the ability to inflict punishment at low cost (high punishment effectiveness) and the presence of a sufficient fraction of strong reciprocators can induce cooperation among self-interested agents, raising group fitness. The model predicts that punishment increases group efficiency, especially when the cost to punishers is low and the impact on defectors is high. Laboratory evidence is reviewed to support these claims, showing that allowing punishment in public goods experiments prevents the decay of cooperation and increases average contributions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public goods games increases group efficiency, especially when punishment is effective (high impact per cost) and not too costly for punishers. The model identifies key moderators: group size, punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and the probability of future interaction. The reviewed experimental evidence (e.g., Fehr & Gächter 2000) shows that punishment prevents the decline of cooperation and can even increase it to near full cooperation, implying a positive effect on efficiency. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that punishment will have a more positive effect on efficiency when the punishment technology is strong and the cost is low, and that group size and the structure of repeated interaction also matter.

- source: Wolff, I. (2012). Retaliation and the role for punishment in the evolution of cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated public goods game with punishment and the possibility of retaliation, punishment can help induce partially cooperative equilibria and temporarily increase cooperation and group payoff, especially when mutation is present. However, punishment cannot sustain full cooperation in the presence of even small mutation rates, as the costs of punishment and the threat of retaliation allow non-punishing cooperators and defectors to invade. The efficiency gains from punishment are thus limited and parameter-dependent: punishment is most effective at intermediate continuation probabilities and when retaliation is not too strong. At high continuation probabilities or when retaliation is easy, punishment is driven out and cannot maintain high efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the efficiency effect of punishment in repeated public goods games is highly sensitive to the possibility of retaliation and the evolutionary dynamics of strategy mutation. When predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, one should consider whether retaliation is possible, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the continuation probability (analogous to number of rounds or game length), and the presence of mutation or experimentation. Punishment can increase efficiency in some cases, but its effect is undermined by retaliation and high continuation probability. The model suggests that punishment is most likely to improve efficiency when it is effective (high punishment impact per cost), retaliation is costly or rare, and the game is not too long. If retaliation is easy and the game is long, punishment may not improve and may even reduce efficiency.

- source: Rand, D. G., Armao, J. J., Nakamaru, M., & Ohtsuki, H. (2010). Anti-social punishment can prevent the co-evolution of punishment and cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in both well-mixed and spatially structured populations, allowing anti-social punishment (punishment of cooperators) prevents the evolution of cooperation in public goods games with costly punishment. In models where only pro-social punishment is possible, cooperation and punishment can co-evolve, leading to high group efficiency. However, when anti-social punishment is included, defecting strategies that punish others become dominant, and cooperation is not stable. This result holds across a wide range of parameter values and is robust to small behavioral errors. The findings highlight that the efficiency gains from punishment depend critically on the absence of anti-social punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is highly contingent on the type of punishment allowed. If anti-social punishment is possible, enabling punishment can reduce or eliminate efficiency gains, as cooperation is not stable and defectors who punish others dominate. In contrast, if only pro-social punishment is possible, enabling punishment can increase efficiency by supporting cooperation. For prediction tasks, the presence or absence of anti-social punishment as a design dimension is critical: if anti-social punishment is allowed, do not expect efficiency gains from punishment; if it is not allowed, efficiency gains are possible under the right parameter regimes.

- source: Henrich, J., & Boyd, R. (2001). Why people punish defectors - Weak conformist transmission can stabilize costly enforcement of norms in cooperative dilemmas. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods dilemmas with costly punishment, the combination of payoff-biased and conformist social learning can stabilize both punishment and cooperation, even when punishment is costly and higher-order free-riding is possible. The model demonstrates that, for a finite number of punishment stages, an arbitrarily small amount of conformist transmission is sufficient to stabilize cooperation and punishment, leading to higher group payoffs. The expected payoff of individuals in cooperative groups is strictly higher than in non-cooperative groups, and these high-payoff equilibria can spread through cultural group selection. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation and punishment are stable and when prosocial genetic traits may invade.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling costly punishment in public goods games can increase group efficiency, provided that some mechanism (such as conformist transmission) supports the stability of punishment. The model clarifies that the efficiency gain from punishment depends on the costs of cooperation and punishment, the error rate, and the strength of conformist transmission. It also highlights that, in the absence of punishment, cooperation is not stable and efficiency is low. Thus, when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, the presence of social learning mechanisms and the cost structure are critical. The model is most informative for settings where group size is large, punishment is costly but effective, and social learning is plausible.

- source: Oya, G., & Ohtsuki, H. (2017). Stable polymorphism of cooperators and punishers in a public goods game. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in well-mixed populations, punishment does not maintain cooperation or improve efficiency; free riders dominate unless initial conditions favor punishers, and even then, cooperation is lost. In spatially structured populations (Wright's island model and stepping stone model), if punishment is sufficiently efficient and the cost of cooperation is less than the cost of punishment, a stable coexistence of cooperators, punishers, and free riders can emerge, either as a limit cycle or a stable equilibrium (especially with mutation). In these cases, the presence of punishment can support higher average payoffs and group welfare than in the absence of punishment. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus context-dependent: it can be positive in structured populations with the right parameters, but is neutral or negative in well-mixed populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on population structure, the relative costs of cooperation and punishment, and the efficiency of punishment. In well-mixed (random matching) environments, enabling punishment does not improve efficiency or maintain cooperation. In spatially structured environments, punishment can support higher efficiency and stable coexistence of strategies, but only if punishment is efficient and costly enough relative to cooperation. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be positive only in structured populations with high punishment efficiency and when cooperation is less costly than punishment. The model also highlights the importance of mutation and the inability to both cooperate and punish in the same round. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in theoretical or agent-based models, and provide mechanistic insight for interpreting empirical results.

- source: Hauser, O. P., Nowak, M. A., & Rand, D. G. (2014). Punishment does not promote cooperation under exploration dynamics when anti-social punishment is possible. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in both compulsory and voluntary public goods games, when all forms of punishment are allowed (including anti-social punishment), the introduction of punishment does not increase the average payoff or efficiency of the group under high mutation rates. Analytical results and agent-based simulations show that the average payoff of cooperators is not increased by punishment when anti-social punishment is possible; in fact, the outcome is equivalent to the no-punishment baseline. Only when punishment is restricted to pro-social forms (punishing defectors only) does punishment increase efficiency. These findings are robust to changes in mutation rate and selection intensity.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that in PGG-like environments with high exploration/mutation and the possibility of anti-social punishment, enabling punishment will not increase group efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition. The effect of punishment on efficiency is neutral or negative when anti-social punishment is possible, regardless of other game parameters such as player count, rounds, or selection intensity. If the game design allows anti-social punishment, do not expect efficiency gains from enabling punishment.

- source: Deng, K. Y., Li, Z. Z., Kurokawa, S., & Chu, T. G. (2012). Rare but severe concerted punishment that favors cooperation. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that implementing rare but severe concerted punishment in a Public Goods game can make cooperation evolutionarily stable and increase group efficiency. The threshold probability for punishment to be effective decreases as the severity of punishment increases and as the population size increases. The total cost of inhibiting defection (i.e., the sum of punishment costs) also decreases with more severe punishment and larger populations. Simulations confirm that the mechanism can spread and that cooperation can become fixed with a probability greater than neutral selection, provided enough groups adopt the mechanism. The model predicts that, compared to standard costly punishment, concerted punishment can achieve higher efficiency and lower cost, especially in large groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in Public Goods games, enabling a rare but severe concerted punishment mechanism (where punishment is shared and severe but infrequent) can substantially increase efficiency compared to no-punishment or standard costly punishment. The effect is especially pronounced in larger groups and with higher punishment severity. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, when the game design includes concerted punishment (shared cost, severe impact, probabilistic triggering), one should expect a significant increase in efficiency relative to the control. The model also provides explicit formulas for the threshold probability and cost-efficiency tradeoffs, which can be used to inform quantitative predictions based on game parameters such as player count, group size, punishment cost, and punishment effectiveness.

- source: Fang, Y. H., Perc, M., & Xu, H. Y. (2020). The Singaporean model in public goods dilemmas with benevolent leaders and bribery. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that exclusion-based punishment by benevolent leaders, especially when leaders are incentivized by group performance (the Singaporean model), can sustain cooperation and high group efficiency, overcoming the second-order free-rider problem. However, as the cost of exclusion increases, or as bribery becomes more prevalent, the ability of punishment to sustain cooperation and efficiency declines, leading to possible collapse into all-defector states. Increasing the performance-based incentive for leaders (parameter alpha) raises the threshold at which punishment remains effective. Bribery undermines the effectiveness of punishment, but its negative impact can be mitigated if the number of bribers is kept low relative to leaders or if the cost of bribery is high.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games depends critically on the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the presence of performance-based incentives for punishers, and the prevalence and cost of corruption/bribery. When punishment is low-cost and/or leaders are strongly incentivized by group performance, enabling punishment increases efficiency. When punishment is costly or easily undermined by bribery, enabling punishment may not improve and can even reduce efficiency. For prediction, the model suggests that game designs with low punishment cost, high punishment effectiveness, and strong performance incentives for punishers will see positive efficiency effects from punishment, while high costs or high corruption will see little or negative effect.

- source: Wang, X. J., & Lv, S. J. (2019). The public goods game with shared punishment cost in well-mixed and structured populations. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing punishment with shared cost among cooperators in a public goods game can promote and stabilize cooperation, thereby increasing group efficiency and average payoff, provided the penalty imposed on defectors is large enough compared to the punishment cost. In well-mixed populations, this can transform the game from one dominated by defection to one where full cooperation is stable. In structured populations, cooperation can emerge at lower fine thresholds and coexist with defection, but as the penalty increases, well-mixed populations become more favorable for cooperation than structured ones. The effect of punishment is thus generally positive for efficiency, but the benefit depends on the relative size of the fine, the punishment cost, and the group size.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that, in public goods games, enabling punishment (with shared cost among cooperators) increases efficiency and group payoff when the fine is sufficiently large relative to the cost. The effect is modulated by group size (player_count), punishment cost (punishment_cost), and punishment effectiveness (punishment_tech, i.e., fine per cost). Structured populations (networked interactions) can allow cooperation to emerge at lower fine thresholds, but as the fine increases, well-mixed populations yield higher efficiency. The findings support the prediction that, for a given control efficiency, enabling punishment will increase efficiency, especially when the punishment is effective and not too costly. The model is theoretical, so quantitative predictions should be interpreted as qualitative guidance.

- source: Huang, F., Chen, X. J., & Wang, L. (2018). Evolution of cooperation in a hierarchical society with corruption control. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a hierarchical public goods game, the presence of corrupt punishment can maintain civilian cooperation but undermines altruistic punishment and reduces population mean payoff. Introducing corruption control, where altruistic punishers can also punish or exclude corrupt punishers, increases both cooperation and efficiency. Asymmetrical punishment (social exclusion of corrupt punishers) is more effective than symmetrical punishment (equal fines for corrupt punishers and defectors) in promoting both cooperation and population mean payoff. The model provides explicit formulas and phase diagrams showing how efficiency depends on group size, punishment cost, fine, bribe, cop proportion, and the probability of spiteful corruption. The results are robust across a range of parameter values.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, quantitative theoretical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games with hierarchical roles and corruption. It shows that the introduction of punishment (especially with corruption control) increases efficiency, and that the effectiveness depends on the design dimensions such as group size, punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and the structure of punishment (symmetrical vs. asymmetrical). The explicit formulas and phase diagrams can be used to estimate treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game parameters. The results are most applicable to settings with hierarchical roles and the possibility of corrupt punishment, but the general findings about the positive effect of well-designed punishment on efficiency are likely to transfer to standard PGGs with punishment.

- source: Zhang, J. L., Chu, T. G., & Weissing, F. J. (2013). Does insurance against punishment undermine cooperation in the evolution of public goods games?. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper models a public goods game with punishment and an additional option for defectors to buy insurance that covers their punishment costs. It shows that if insurance is cheaper than the expected punishment, cooperation can be destabilized, leading to lower group efficiency and welfare. However, if insurance is realistically priced (at or above the expected fine), cooperation remains stable and efficient. The results are presented as a set of scenarios, with the stability of cooperation, defection, or speculation depending on the relative costs of cooperation, punishment, and insurance. The main implication is that punishment remains an effective mechanism for sustaining cooperation and efficiency unless insurance is unrealistically cheap.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the efficiency gains from punishment in public goods games are robust to the introduction of insurance against punishment, as long as the insurance is not priced below the expected punishment cost. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in standard public goods games with punishment, efficiency should remain high unless there are additional mechanisms (like cheap insurance) that allow defectors to avoid punishment at a lower cost. The model also clarifies that the effectiveness of punishment (and thus its impact on efficiency) depends on the relationship between the cost of cooperation, the expected punishment, and any available insurance. If the control game (without punishment) is inefficient, introducing punishment should increase efficiency unless insurance is unrealistically cheap.

- source: Powers, S. T., Taylor, D. J., & Bryson, J. J. (2012). Punishment can promote defection in group-structured populations. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in linear public goods games with group structure, the addition of anti-social punishment (defectors punishing cooperators) can prevent the evolution and maintenance of pro-social punishment and cooperation. This reduces the mean fitness and group payoff that would otherwise be achieved through cooperation and pro-social punishment. The effect is stronger in larger groups and when dispersal is less frequent. The presence of anti-social punishment raises the threshold for pro-social punishment to be stable, requiring greater between-group variance (e.g., smaller group sizes) for cooperation to persist. Thus, anti-social punishment makes it less likely that punishment will improve efficiency in group-structured populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on the possibility of anti-social punishment and on group structure parameters such as group size and dispersal frequency. When anti-social punishment is possible, enabling punishment may not increase efficiency and can even reduce it, especially in larger groups or when between-group variance is low. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of anti-social punishment, larger group sizes, and less frequent dispersal all reduce the likelihood that punishment will improve efficiency. The paper also clarifies that the effect-to-cost ratio of punishment and cooperation modulate these effects, but the negative impact of anti-social punishment is robust across a range of parameter values.

- source: Ishikawa, B. Y. S., & Fontanari, J. F. (2025). Revisiting institutional punishment in the N-person prisoner's dilemma. *Theory in Biosciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional punishment in public goods games can stabilize high-efficiency (all-punishers) equilibria, but only if the cost to punishers is sufficiently low relative to the fines imposed on non-punishers and defectors. Cost sharing among punishers (i.e., dividing the maintenance cost of the institution) expands the parameter region where high efficiency is possible and increases the basin of attraction for the all-punishers equilibrium. However, the all-defectors equilibrium (zero efficiency) is always stable, so initial conditions and parameter settings are crucial. The group size N and the minimum number of punishers required to establish the institution (K_m) both affect the threshold frequency of punishers needed for high efficiency. In finite populations, demographic noise can sometimes allow cooperators to fixate, but this does not generally lead to robust high-efficiency outcomes. The model does not include optional participation, peer punishment, or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence for how institutional punishment, cost sharing, group size, and punishment cost parameters affect the efficiency of public goods games. For the prediction task, it shows that enabling institutional punishment can increase efficiency dramatically, but only if the punishment cost is not too high relative to the fines and the group can reach a critical mass of punishers. Cost sharing (lower per-punisher cost as more punishers join) makes high efficiency more likely. Larger groups require a higher absolute number of punishers, but the threshold frequency can vary non-monotonically with group size. If the control game (no punishment) is at low efficiency (all-defectors), enabling punishment can, under the right conditions, shift the system to high efficiency, but only if the cost/benefit ratios and initial conditions are favorable. The paper does not address peer punishment, rewards, or communication, so its findings are most applicable to institutional punishment in large, well-mixed groups.

- source: Eldakar, O. T., Gallup, A. C., & Driscoll, W. W. (2013). WHEN HAWKS GIVE RISE TO DOVES: THE EVOLUTION AND TRANSITION OF ENFORCEMENT STRATEGIES. *Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that punishment can increase group efficiency (fitness) in public goods game environments, but the pathway depends on the type of punisher. Selfish punishers can invade selfish populations and reduce cheating, thereby increasing group payoff and paving the way for altruistic punishers, who further increase group efficiency. The effectiveness of punishment in raising group payoff is higher when punishment costs are low, group size is small, and the number of rounds (iterations) is high. The model directly analyzes how these design dimensions affect the ability of punishment to increase group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment in a public goods game can increase group efficiency, especially when punishment costs are low, group size is small, and the number of rounds is high. The model is directly based on a standard PGG and manipulates key design dimensions, making its findings highly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results suggest that the efficiency gain from punishment is larger when punishment is less costly and when group structure allows for stronger group selection (smaller groups, longer association). The findings are most applicable to settings where punishment leads to exclusion or strong consequences for defectors.

- source: Mathew, S., & Boyd, R. (2009). When does optional participation allow the evolution of cooperation?. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that optional participation only allows punishment to invade and stabilize cooperation (and thus increase efficiency) under specific conditions: (1) loners must have higher payoffs than defectors, (2) two cooperators must have higher per capita payoffs than loners, and (3) the public good must have constant or increasing returns to scale. If these conditions are not met, the system does not support oscillations that allow punishment to invade, and the long-run outcome is either all defectors or all loners, both of which are inefficient. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the game's payoff structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games with optional participation is not universal, but depends on specific game design dimensions: the relative payoffs of loners, defectors, and cooperators, and the returns to scale in public goods production. For prediction, this means that knowing the control efficiency is not sufficient; one must also know whether the game's payoff structure meets the three key conditions identified. If so, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency; if not, punishment may have little or no effect. The paper does not provide quantitative predictions but offers clear qualitative moderators.

- source: Powers, S. T., Perret, C., & Currie, T. E. (2023). Playing the political game: the coevolution of institutions with group size and political inequality. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that institutionally arranged punishment in public goods games allows groups to sustain high levels of cooperation and achieve much larger group sizes (higher efficiency/welfare) than groups without such institutions. As groups grow, the cost of forming consensus over rules increases, but the evolution of hierarchy (a skewed distribution of influence) allows large groups to maintain efficient cooperation-promoting institutions by reducing the cost of consensus formation. Groups that invest just enough in punishment to deter defection, while allocating the rest to technology (increasing carrying capacity), achieve the highest efficiency. If hierarchy cannot evolve, large groups cannot sustain cooperation and remain small and inefficient. The model also shows that if the political game (consensus formation) is too difficult, groups may fail to maintain cooperation-promoting institutions, leading to lower efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public goods games increases group efficiency, especially as group size increases, provided that institutional rules are set optimally (not over- or under-investing in punishment). The model also highlights that the cost of consensus formation (political game) can limit the efficiency gains from punishment in large groups unless hierarchy evolves to reduce these costs. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is strongly moderated by group size, the cost of institutional rule formation, and the ability to evolve or implement hierarchy. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where punishment is institutionally arranged and the costs of monitoring/sanctioning are covered by the group, rather than being individually altruistic.

- source: Sasaki, T., & Uchida, S. (2013). The evolution of cooperation by social exclusion. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that social exclusion is a more effective and robust mechanism than costly punishment for sustaining cooperation and high efficiency in public goods games. When excluders can effectively and cheaply exclude defectors, the group can reach and maintain the efficient outcome where all contribute and defectors are eliminated. Costly punishment, even when effective, is vulnerable to invasion by non-punishing cooperators (second-order freeriders), leading to a decline in punishers and eventual dominance by defectors, which collapses efficiency. The model demonstrates that social exclusion can solve both the emergence and maintenance problems of cooperation, provided the cost of exclusion is not too high. The results are robust to changes in group size and to stochastic effects. The paper also notes that if monitoring costs are high, or if exclusion is not self-serving, the advantage of social exclusion disappears.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in public goods games, enabling a punishment mechanism that takes the form of social exclusion (where defectors are excluded from benefit sharing and the cost of exclusion is not too high) will lead to a large increase in group efficiency compared to the control (no punishment) condition. The effect is robust across group sizes and does not require repeated interaction or reputation. In contrast, enabling costly punishment (where punishers pay to reduce defectors' payoffs) is less likely to increase efficiency, especially in the long run, due to vulnerability to second-order freeriders. For prediction tasks, the presence of a social exclusion mechanism with low cost and high effectiveness should be treated as a strong positive moderator of the efficiency effect of punishment. The specific model parameters (group size, multiplication factor, cost of exclusion/punishment, effectiveness) are all explicitly analyzed, allowing for quantitative predictions if mapped to empirical settings.

- source: Adami, C., Schossau, J., & Hintze, A. (2016). Evolutionary game theory using agent-based methods. *Physics of Life Reviews*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in agent-based simulations of the public goods game, the introduction of punishment (where cooperators can punish defectors at a cost) shifts the critical synergy parameter (r) required for cooperation to be stable, thus expanding the parameter region where cooperation (and thus higher group efficiency) is observed. The effect is quantitatively predicted by mean-field theory and confirmed by simulations: higher probability or density of punishment lowers the threshold for cooperation. However, because punishment is costly, it creates a second-order dilemma, and the evolutionary dynamics can lead to meta-stable states and hysteresis. When punishment is allowed to evolve, its effect is strongest near the critical point, and in highly cooperative or highly defective regimes, punishment is not maintained by selection. The findings are robust across different group sizes and mutation rates, and the paper provides explicit equations and simulation results for the effect of punishment on group payoff and cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in public goods games, enabling punishment increases group efficiency (payoff) in the dilemma region by shifting the critical point for cooperation to lower values of the synergy parameter (r). The effect size and direction depend on the punishment parameters (cost, effectiveness), group size, and whether punishment is fixed or can evolve. For the downstream prediction task, the paper supports the prediction that, controlling for game design dimensions and baseline efficiency, enabling punishment will generally increase efficiency, especially near the cooperation/defection threshold. The paper also highlights that the effect of punishment is context-dependent: it is most effective near the critical point, and less so when cooperation or defection is already dominant. The explicit equations and simulation results can inform quantitative predictions.

- source: Schoenmakers, S., Hilbe, C., Blasius, B., & Traulsen, A. (2014). Sanctions as honest signals - The evolution of pool punishment by public sanctioning institutions. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that public sanctioning institutions (pool punishment) can evolve and sustain high levels of cooperation and group efficiency in public goods games, provided their existence is publicly visible. The signaling effect of such institutions deters free-riding and enables opportunistic cooperation, leading to a stable equilibrium with a mix of pool punishers and opportunists. This equilibrium achieves higher average payoffs than regimes without visible punishment or without the option to abstain. Diminishing taxes (cost-sharing among tax payers) further increase efficiency, while second-order punishment eliminates tax evasion but does not increase average payoffs beyond the baseline with only first-order punishment. The efficiency of the punishment technology and the level of public information about the institution are critical for the emergence and stability of efficient cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence and public visibility of a punishment institution in a PGG-like environment increases group efficiency (average payoff) compared to control conditions without punishment. The model allows direct mapping to game design dimensions such as player count, punishment cost, punishment technology, and information structure. It predicts that enabling punishment (with public visibility) will increase efficiency, especially when the punishment is sufficiently deterrent (fine > cost of cooperation + tax), and when the institution is visible to most or all players. Diminishing the per-person cost of punishment (e.g., via cost-sharing) further increases efficiency. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, given knowledge of the control efficiency and the game design parameters.

- source: Dercole, F., De Carli, M., Della Rossa, F., & Papadopoulos, A. V. (2013). Overpunishing is not necessary to fix cooperation in voluntary public goods games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both peer-punishment (overpunishing) and shared-punishment (fixed sanction per defector, cost shared among punishers) in voluntary PGGs can lead to the fixation of cooperation and high group efficiency, provided antisocial punishment is absent. The main difference is quantitative: overpunishing increases the fraction of initial conditions leading to full cooperation, but shared-punishment is sufficient to achieve efficient outcomes in most cases, especially when stochasticity (finite populations) is considered. The likelihood of efficient cooperation increases with group size, sanction severity, and the public goods multiplier. The paper provides explicit payoff formulas and analyzes the basins of attraction for efficient (cooperative) equilibria under a wide range of parameter settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment in voluntary PGGs increases group efficiency, even when punishment is mild (shared-punishment). The effect is robust across a wide range of group sizes, round numbers, and parameter values. The paper gives explicit formulas for payoffs and the conditions under which cooperation (and thus efficiency) is achieved. For prediction tasks, the presence of voluntary participation, absence of antisocial punishment, and the ability to punish defectors (even with fixed, shared-cost sanctions) are sufficient to expect a large increase in efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline. The effect is stronger with larger groups, higher punishment severity, and higher public goods multipliers. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in PGG-like games with similar design features.

- source: Archetti, M., & Scheuring, I. (2013). Trading public goods stabilizes interspecific mutualism. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with non-linear (sigmoid or step) benefit functions, stable mutualism and high group efficiency can be achieved without punishment, even in large groups and with high costs. Punishment is only necessary to maintain mutualism and efficiency in the special case of linear benefits (the N-person prisoner's dilemma). When punishment is introduced in the linear case, it drives the system to full cooperation and maximal efficiency. In non-linear cases, punishment is not required for high efficiency, as the non-linearity itself stabilizes cooperation at high levels. The paper provides analytical and numerical results for the equilibrium frequency of cooperators and the conditions for stable mutualism.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on the shape of the benefit function. If the game is linear (standard PGG), enabling punishment will have a strong positive effect on efficiency, driving the group to full cooperation. If the game is non-linear (e.g., threshold, sigmoid, or step), high efficiency can be achieved without punishment, and enabling punishment adds little or no additional benefit. Therefore, when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, it is crucial to know whether the benefit function is linear or non-linear. Other game design dimensions (group size, cost/benefit ratio) interact with this, but the linearity of the benefit is the key moderator identified by this paper.

- source: Archetti, M., Scheuring, I., Hoffman, M., Frederickson, M. E., Pierce, N. E., & Yu, D. W. (2011). Economic game theory for mutualism and cooperation. *Ecology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in nonlinear public goods games (which are more biologically realistic than linear ones), a mixed equilibrium with both cooperators and defectors can exist, and group payoff (efficiency) can be substantial and close to maximum even without assortment or repeated interactions. The effect of punishment or sanctions (host sanctions) is formally distinguished from partner fidelity feedback, with different evolutionary implications. The models show that the shape of the public good function (nonlinearity) is a key moderator of efficiency and cooperation, and that punishment/sanctions can maintain cooperation, but the resulting equilibrium is generally not fully efficient. The paper does not present new empirical data but provides a theoretical framework for interpreting efficiency and punishment effects in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting efficiency in public goods games as a function of game design dimensions, especially the shape of the public good function (linearity vs. nonlinearity), group size, and cost/benefit ratio. It clarifies that punishment/sanctions (host sanctions) can support cooperation and increase group payoff, but the efficiency gain depends on whether the mechanism is true punishment (requiring commitment) or partner fidelity feedback (self-serving host response). The models suggest that in nonlinear public goods games, efficiency can be high even without punishment, and the marginal effect of punishment may be limited by the structure of the game. The paper is highly relevant for interpreting the likely efficiency effects of punishment in both linear and nonlinear public goods games, but does not provide direct empirical estimates.

- source: Milinski, M., & Rockenbach, B. (2012). On the interaction of the stick and the carrot in social dilemmas. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that allowing punishment in public goods games reliably increases contributions and, over time, can increase group efficiency, especially when combined with reputation or indirect reciprocity mechanisms. Punishment is more effective than reward alone in sustaining cooperation and efficiency. However, the costs of punishment can initially reduce efficiency, and only after repeated interactions does the net group payoff surpass the no-punishment baseline. When both punishment and reputation (indirect reciprocity) are available, efficiency is higher than with punishment alone, as the need for costly punishment is reduced. The review also notes that the effectiveness of punishment depends on its cost-effectiveness ratio and that the presence of reward does not always further increase efficiency unless it is combined with punishment in a way that targets norm violators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public goods games increases efficiency relative to control, especially when punishment is effective and not prohibitively costly. The efficiency gains are most pronounced when punishment is combined with reputation or indirect reciprocity mechanisms, which reduce the frequency and cost of punishment needed. The review highlights the importance of game design dimensions such as punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and the presence of reward or reputation mechanisms. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the efficiency boost from punishment is moderated by these dimensions, and that combining punishment with reputation mechanisms yields the highest efficiency.

- source: Guala, F. (2012). Reciprocity: Weak or strong? What punishment experiments do (and do not) demonstrate. *Behavioral and Brain Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that laboratory experiments with public goods games and punishment show that while punishment can increase cooperation rates, it often reduces group efficiency due to the destruction of resources. Efficiency gains from punishment are only observed under specific conditions, such as very long repeated interactions with the same group, and even then, the need for punishment diminishes as cooperation stabilizes. In field and ethnographic studies, there is little evidence for the use of costly, uncoordinated punishment to sustain cooperation; instead, low- or no-cost sanctions (gossip, ostracism, coordinated punishment) are prevalent and effective. The paper argues that the external validity of laboratory punishment experiments is limited, and that real-world institutions that sustain cooperation do so by reducing the cost of punishment, extending the time horizon, and improving information flow.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that enabling costly, uncoordinated punishment in public goods games is unlikely to increase efficiency except under narrow conditions (e.g., long repeated games with stable groups). In most laboratory settings, punishment increases cooperation but reduces efficiency due to resource destruction. In real-world or field-like settings, cooperation is more effectively sustained by low-cost, coordinated, or symbolic sanctions, not by costly punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper implies that the presence of costly punishment (especially if uncoordinated and not paired with communication or institutional support) should not be expected to reliably increase efficiency over the control condition, and may even reduce it. Game design dimensions that reduce the cost of punishment, allow communication, or enable coordination are likely to moderate the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Chen, X. J., & Perc, M. (2014). Optimal distribution of incentives for public cooperation in heterogeneous interaction environments. *Frontiers in Behavioral Neuroscience*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games on heterogeneous (scale-free) networks, the optimal way to distribute limited institutional punishment (and reward) depends on the network structure, the enhancement factor (r), and whether payoffs are absolute or degree-normalized. For low enhancement factors, distributing punishment/reward equally is optimal for maximizing cooperation. For high enhancement factors, targeting high-degree nodes (punishing or rewarding them more) is optimal, but the direction depends on whether payoffs are absolute or normalized. The main outcome is the stationary fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games, especially in heterogeneous network settings. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency) depends on the distribution of punishment, the enhancement factor (analogous to MPCR), and the network structure. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited to inferring that higher cooperation rates (under optimal punishment schemes) may translate to higher efficiency, especially when the enhancement factor is high. The findings are most relevant for settings with network heterogeneity and institutional (not peer) punishment.

- source: Baker, F., & Rachlin, H. (2002). Teaching and learning in a probabilistic prisoner's dilemma. *Behavioural Processes*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing punishment or reward mechanisms—whether applied individually (peer-to-peer) or collectively (by group consensus)—substantially increases contributions in public goods games. The effect is stronger as the penalty/reward size or effectiveness increases, and as the proportion of punishers/rewarders in the population rises. Both mechanisms are effective, but punishment is found to be marginally more effective than reward in promoting contributions. The model also shows that collective mechanisms (pool punishment/reward) can be more effective than individual ones under certain parameter regimes. Although efficiency is not directly measured, the payoff structure implies that higher contributions lead to higher group payoffs, except for the initial cost to punishers/rewarders. The findings are robust across a range of parameter values and network structures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in a public goods game will increase group contributions and, by implication, group efficiency/payoff, relative to a control without punishment. The model explores a wide range of parameter values for punishment cost, effectiveness, and the proportion of punishers, showing that higher punishment effectiveness and more punishers lead to higher contributions. While the paper does not report efficiency as a ratio, the payoff equations and discussion make it clear that group payoff increases with contribution. The results are most directly applicable to networked, repeated PGGs with binary (cooperate/defect) choices and both individual and collective punishment/reward options. The findings should inform predictions that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment will yield higher efficiency, especially when punishment is effective and not prohibitively costly.

- source: Podder, S., Righi, S., & Pancotto, F. (2021). Reputation and punishment sustain cooperation in the optional public goods game. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in the OPGG, punishment alone (including anti-social punishment) does not increase cooperation or group fitness, and can even reduce them by increasing the prevalence of loners. Reputation alone can increase cooperation and fitness, but only under specific social norms (e.g., when defectors are assigned a strictly worse reputation than loners). The combination of reputation and punishment leads to the highest levels of cooperation and population fitness, with most agents adopting conditional cooperation strategies and rarely using punishment. The results are robust to variations in group size, synergy factor, punishment cost and effectiveness, and other parameters. The findings suggest that reputation and punishment interact synergistically to sustain cooperation and improve group outcomes, but that reputation can substitute for costly punishment in many cases.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in optional public goods games, the addition of punishment alone (especially when anti-social punishment is possible) does not reliably increase efficiency or group payoff, and may even reduce it. However, when reputation mechanisms are also present—especially those that allow agents to distinguish defectors from loners—punishment and reputation together can synergistically increase cooperation and group fitness. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency depends critically on the presence and nature of reputation mechanisms and social norms. In the absence of reputation, enabling punishment may not increase efficiency; with reputation, especially under certain norms, enabling punishment can have a more positive effect. The model's robustness checks indicate these findings are not highly sensitive to group size, rounds, or punishment cost within the tested ranges.

- source: Janssen, M. A., & Bushman, C. (2008). Evolution of cooperation and altruistic punishment when retaliation is possible. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that when agents in a public goods game can retaliate against those who punish them, the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation is greatly reduced. In the absence of retaliation, punishment can support high levels of cooperation (and thus higher group payoffs/efficiency). When retaliation is possible, cooperation and punishment both decline, especially in larger groups or when the cost of retaliation is low relative to the cost of punishment. The visibility of the punisher (i.e., whether punished agents can identify who punished them) is also important: if punishers are less visible, retaliation is less likely and cooperation can be higher. The model also shows that spiteful punishment (defectors punishing others unconditionally) does not undermine the main result: retaliation is the key factor that reduces cooperation and, by implication, efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the efficiency gains from enabling punishment in public goods games are highly contingent on whether retaliation is possible and on the relative costs of punishment and retaliation. In environments where punished agents can easily identify and retaliate against punishers, the positive effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be much smaller or even negative, especially in larger groups. The model also highlights the importance of punisher anonymity: making it harder to identify punishers can help preserve the efficiency benefits of punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of retaliation and punisher identifiability are critical moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency.

- source: Sigmund, K. (2007). Punish or perish? Retaliation and collaboration among humans. *Trends in Ecology & Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of punishment in public goods games increases cooperation and contributions, but the cost of punishment can reduce average group income, at least initially. Over time, as cooperation becomes the norm and punishment is less frequently used, the group can approach efficient outcomes. However, the efficiency gains depend on the cost-effectiveness of punishment and the dynamics of the group. The review also notes that rewards alone are less effective than punishment in boosting cooperation, but combining indirect reciprocity (reputation-based rewards) with punishment can increase efficiency. Voluntary participation in public goods games with punishment can also enhance cooperation and efficiency compared to compulsory participation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and review-based support for the idea that punishment increases cooperation in public goods games, but its effect on efficiency is mixed and context-dependent. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment is likely to increase contributions, but whether this translates into higher efficiency depends on the cost of punishment, the number of rounds, and whether cooperation stabilizes. If punishment is costly and used frequently, efficiency may decrease; if it is used mainly as a threat and actual punishment becomes rare, efficiency can increase. The review also suggests that combining punishment with reputation-based rewards or allowing voluntary participation can improve efficiency. Thus, when predicting efficiency with punishment enabled, consider not only the presence of punishment but also its cost, the game structure, and whether mechanisms exist to reduce the frequency or cost of punishment over time.

- source: Brandt, H., Hauert, C., & Sigmund, K. (2003). Punishment and reputation in spatial public goods games. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in spatial public goods games with three players, the introduction of punishment opportunities transforms the system from one where cooperators and defectors can coexist (with cooperation only possible above a relatively high threshold r_c ≈ 2.07) to one where the threshold for stable cooperation is much lower (r_c ≈ 1.35 with punishment, and r_c ≈ 1.25 with both punishment and reputation). This means that punishment enables cooperative, group-beneficial outcomes under a wider range of game parameters. The presence of punishment eliminates coexistence and leads to bistability: either a fully cooperative or a fully defecting state, depending on initial conditions. Reputation further enhances cooperation and fairness by making the cooperative state more robust and reducing the prevalence of non-punishing cooperators. The findings are based on evolutionary game-theoretic modeling and simulations, not on laboratory or field experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in spatial public goods games increases the likelihood of efficient, cooperative outcomes, as measured by the range of parameters (specifically, the multiplication factor r) under which cooperation is stable. The reduction in the threshold r_c for cooperation is a clear signal that punishment makes high-efficiency outcomes possible in settings where they would otherwise not occur. While the paper does not report efficiency as a direct ratio of actual to maximal group payoff, the shift in r_c is a strong proxy for increased group welfare. The findings are most applicable to spatial, all-or-nothing, three-player, repeated games with binary contribution and punishment choices, and may be less directly transferable to well-mixed or continuous-contribution settings.

- source: Helbing, D., Szolnoki, A., Perc, M., & Szabo, G. (2010). Evolutionary Establishment of Moral and Double Moral Standards through Spatial Interactions. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that costly punishment in spatial public goods games enables the spread and dominance of moralists (cooperators who punish), eliminating both defectors and non-punishing cooperators over time. This resolves the second-order free-rider problem and leads to higher levels of cooperation than would be expected in well-mixed populations, where defectors dominate. The effect depends on the punishment fine-to-cost ratio and the synergy factor (MPCR analog), with higher synergy and/or higher fine-to-cost ratios favoring the spread of moralists. The presence of a small number of defectors can accelerate the spread of moralists. The model shows that spatial structure is crucial: in well-mixed populations, punishment does not prevent the tragedy of the commons, but in spatial settings, punishment can establish and maintain cooperation. While the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, the dominance of moralists and elimination of defectors implies a more efficient outcome than the baseline.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment in spatially structured public goods games can substantially increase group efficiency by promoting the dominance of punishing cooperators (moralists) and eliminating defectors and non-punishing cooperators. The effect is sensitive to the punishment fine-to-cost ratio and the synergy factor (MPCR), but is robust to group size and network structure as long as spatial clustering exists. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (with sufficient effectiveness and not too high cost) in a spatial or networked setting should be expected to increase efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition, especially when the control is dominated by defectors. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency, so predictions should be qualitative or based on the expected dominance of cooperative strategies.

- source: Flores, L. S., Fernandes, H. C. M., Amaral, M. A., & Vainstein, M. H. (2021). Symbiotic behaviour in the public goods game with altruistic punishment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial PGG, the introduction of altruistic punishment allows for the survival of cooperation in a wider range of parameters than in the classic PGG. For low punishment costs, clusters of punishers can survive and support cooperation. At intermediate punishment costs, both punishers and cooperators are vulnerable to defectors. At high punishment costs, a symbiotic spatial structure emerges where neither cooperators nor punishers can survive alone, but together they can outcompete defectors by forming clusters with punishers in the core and cooperators at the boundary. The effect of punishment on cooperation is thus highly dependent on the cost and the spatial structure. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff directly, but the persistence of altruistic strategies is used as a proxy for cooperative success.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation and, by implication, efficiency in spatial PGGs is highly sensitive to the cost of punishment and the spatial structure of interactions. While it does not report efficiency directly, it shows that punishment can expand the parameter region where cooperation survives, especially when spatial clustering allows for symbiosis between cooperators and punishers. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that punishment is most effective at low cost, can be detrimental at intermediate cost, and can again be effective at high cost if spatial symbiosis is possible. The results are most applicable to spatially structured games with local interactions and may not generalize to well-mixed or non-spatial settings.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2013). Effectiveness of conditional punishment for the evolution of public cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that conditional punishment, where the severity of punishment is proportional to the number of punishers in a group, is more effective than unconditional punishment in promoting cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially when punishment is costly. Conditional punishers can maintain cooperation at lower fines and costs, and are more efficient in terms of cooperation per unit cost. Unconditional punishers only outperform conditional punishers when punishment is very cheap. The results are robust across different values of the multiplication factor (mpcr), and the findings are explained through the mechanism of indirect territorial competition and pattern formation on the spatial grid.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatial public goods games, the design of the punishment mechanism (conditional vs. unconditional) and the cost-to-fine ratio are critical moderators of the effect of punishment on cooperation and cost-effectiveness. When predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, the results suggest that conditional punishment is more likely to yield higher cooperation per unit cost, especially when punishment is not cheap. However, the paper does not directly report group efficiency as a fraction of the maximum possible payoff, so predictions about absolute efficiency should be made with caution.
