The results are derived analytically from payoff and replicator dynamics equations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that increasing group size in public goods games with indirect reciprocity (and no explicit punishment mechanism) makes it much harder for cooperation and high efficiency to evolve. Thus, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in large groups, the baseline efficiency is likely to be low, and the marginal effect of punishment may depend on whether it can overcome the group size barrier. The paper also highlights the importance of the reputation mechanism: if the punishment or sanctioning system is more like 'standing' (justified punishment does not harm reputation), cooperation is more likely to be stable. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency as a function of group size, error rates, and reputation mechanisms, but do not directly model explicit costly punishment as a game option.

- source: Kristensen, N. P., Chisholm, R. A., & Ohtsuki, H. (2025). Many-strategy games in groups with relatives and the evolution of coordinated cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that kin selection (homophily) can facilitate the initial invasion of coordinated cooperation in public goods games with nonlinear (sigmoidal) benefits, even when such cooperation cannot invade in a well-mixed population due to cognitive or coordination costs. Once established, coordinated cooperation can persist as homophily declines, provided that the benefit function is sufficiently nonlinear and the switching gain from non-contribution to contribution at the coordination point exceeds the cost. If this condition is not met, some level of homophily must be maintained to prevent the invasion of defectors or 'liars' (who commit to contribute but do not follow through). The framework provides analytical and numerical tools for predicting evolutionary outcomes (including efficiency and group payoff) as a function of group size, relatedness, benefit function nonlinearity, and strategy set. The results are directly relevant to predicting efficiency in PGGs with kin structure and nonlinear payoffs, but do not address punishment or sanctions directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a general theoretical framework for predicting efficiency and group payoff in public goods games with arbitrary group size, number of strategies, and non-additive (e.g., sigmoidal) benefit functions, under varying levels of kin structure (homophily). It is directly relevant for predicting the efficiency of control games (without punishment) as a function of game design dimensions such as player count, number of rounds, MPCR (via the benefit function), and group structure. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it analyze the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The framework could, in principle, be extended to include punishment as a discrete strategy, but this is not done in the present paper. Therefore, the paper is highly informative for predicting baseline efficiency in PGGs with kin structure and nonlinear payoffs, but provides only indirect or contextual support for predicting the effect of punishment.

- source: Arenas, A., Camacho, J., Cuesta, J. A., & Requejo, R. J. (2011). The joker effect: Cooperation driven by destructive agents. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing destructive agents ('jokers') into a public goods game leads to robust cycles in which cooperation periodically surges. The mechanism is that jokers harm defectors more than cooperators, creating a dynamic where cooperators can invade jokers, jokers invade defectors, and defectors invade cooperators. The cycles are robust to mutation and population size. The model provides explicit payoff expressions for each strategy, but the main focus is on the evolutionary dynamics of strategy frequencies, not on group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are relevant for understanding how destructive threats can promote cooperation, but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in standard PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not study punishment or sanctions per se. Instead, it analyzes the effect of introducing a destructive agent type that harms all non-jokers. The model is a theoretical extension of the PGG and provides insight into how external threats or destructive forces can promote cooperation through population dynamics. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence about efficiency changes due to punishment, nor does it manipulate or analyze standard punishment mechanisms. Its main value is in highlighting that destructive forces can, under certain conditions, increase cooperation, but this is not equivalent to the effect of punishment in PGGs.

- source: Chen, T., Wu, Z. H., & Wang, L. (2018). Disseminators or silencers: The effect of information diffusion intensity on cooperation in public goods game. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the presence and type of information dissemination strongly affect the speed at which full cooperation is achieved in a spatial public goods game with reputation and conformity. Negative disseminators (who spread information about defectors) most often lead to the fastest convergence to full cooperation, followed by neutral and positive disseminators. Silencers (no dissemination) are least effective. The effect of dissemination type is most pronounced when conformity synergy, link-neighbor degree, population size, or environmental noise are high, or when the synergy factor is low. However, all models eventually reach full cooperation, so there is no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare differences between models.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it is informative about the role of information diffusion and reputation in accelerating the achievement of cooperation. The findings suggest that mechanisms which amplify reputational consequences for defectors (akin to negative gossip or public shaming) can speed up the transition to full cooperation, especially in certain parameter regimes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not address whether the final payoffs or efficiency differ between conditions.

- source: Wang, Z., Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2014). Rewarding evolutionary fitness with links between populations promotes cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that rewarding players whose utility exceeds a threshold by granting them external links to another population can significantly promote cooperation in both public goods and prisoner's dilemma games on structured networks. The effect is maximized at intermediate threshold values, where rewarded players are rare but able to percolate, acting as catalysts for cooperation. If the threshold is too low or too high, the effect diminishes. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed. The results are robust across network types and game details. Punishment is not modeled, only discussed as background.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It models a reward mechanism (external links for high-fitness players) and analyzes its effect on cooperation rates, not on efficiency or payoff. While it discusses the relative merits of reward and punishment in the introduction, all results pertain to reward. Therefore, it is not directly informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency, except as context for how reward mechanisms can promote cooperation under certain network and threshold conditions.

- source: Morsky, B., & Akçay, E. (2021). False beliefs can bootstrap cooperative communities through social norms. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that inaccurate, overly optimistic beliefs about the level of cooperation can bootstrap and sustain high cooperation in communities governed by conditional cooperation norms, even in anonymous settings. The model shows that the dynamics of joining, learning, and leaving the community can create bistability, abrupt transitions, or cycles in cooperation rates and community size. High cooperation can be achieved and maintained if naive (optimistic) individuals are present in sufficient numbers, and if learning the true (lower) level of cooperation is slow or if disillusioned individuals can easily leave. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward, but discusses how mechanisms that increase transparency or allow for punishment may interact with these dynamics. The findings are relevant for understanding how social norms and belief management can foster cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how belief management and social norm dynamics can affect cooperation rates in public-goods-game-like environments, especially in the absence of explicit punishment or reward. It suggests that mechanisms which slow learning about true cooperation levels or allow for easy exit can paradoxically increase cooperation by maintaining a pool of optimistic, conditionally cooperative individuals. However, because the model does not include punishment, reward, or explicit efficiency/payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The discussion of punishment is conceptual and highlights possible trade-offs between transparency (which may reduce cooperation by revealing low compliance) and incentive mechanisms (which may increase or decrease cooperation depending on beliefs). For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for understanding the role of belief dynamics and social norms as moderators, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Alvarez-Rodriguez, U., Battiston, F., de Arruda, G. F., Moreno, Y., Perc, M., & Latora, V. (2021). Evolutionary dynamics of higher-order interactions in social networks. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper shows that in PGGs with higher-order interactions (hypergraphs), the critical synergy factor required for cooperation decreases as group size increases in uniform random hypergraphs, meaning larger groups can foster cooperation more easily (at a given synergy factor). Heterogeneity in group membership (hyperdegree) and group size can either facilitate or hinder cooperation, depending on the structure: scale-free (hub-dominated) hypergraphs inhibit cooperation, while uniform or less heterogeneous structures do not. The framework is validated with empirical data from scientific collaborations, showing that the synergy factor (group benefit) is a non-monotonic function of group size, with an optimal group size for maximum benefit. The paper does not study punishment, rewards, or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency and payoff outcomes in PGG-like environments with varying group size, group structure, and synergy factor, but it does not provide any evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions. It is directly relevant for understanding how group size and network heterogeneity affect the critical conditions for cooperation and group payoff, but cannot inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment. Use this paper to inform baseline efficiency predictions in control (no-punishment) conditions as a function of group size, group structure, and synergy factor, but not for treatment effects of punishment.

- source: Archetti, M., & Scheuring, I. (2011). COEXISTENCE OF COOPERATION AND DEFECTION IN PUBLIC GOODS GAMES. *Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of the public goods function (linear vs. nonlinear/threshold) fundamentally changes the equilibrium outcome. In nonlinear/threshold (Volunteer's Dilemma-like) games, cooperation and defection can coexist at a stable mixed equilibrium, and the public good is produced with positive probability, even in one-shot, well-mixed populations without relatedness or repeated play. In linear (NPD) games, only defection is stable in the absence of assortment. The paper provides analytical and numerical results for the equilibrium frequency of cooperation and the probability of public good provision as a function of group size, cost/benefit ratio, and the threshold parameter. No punishment, reward, or external enforcement mechanisms are modeled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in public goods games as a function of the public good production function (linear vs. nonlinear/threshold), group size, and cost/benefit ratio, but it does not address the effects of punishment or sanctions. It provides direct theoretical predictions for efficiency and group payoff in the absence of punishment, but cannot inform the effect of enabling punishment. For downstream prediction tasks focused on the effect of punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it is valuable for understanding baseline efficiency in control conditions without punishment, especially in games with nonlinear or threshold public goods functions.

- source: Reeves, T., Ohtsuki, H., & Fukui, S. (2017). Asymmetric public goods game cooperation through pest control. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in asymmetric public goods games (modeled as pest control among farmers), the Nash equilibrium investment and resulting efficiency depend strongly on the discount factor and the number of players. There are threshold effects: only farmers close to the risk (frontier) contribute when the discount factor is low, while more distant farmers contribute only if the discount factor is high enough. Increasing the number of players does not always increase efficiency; in some parameter regions, adding more players can paradoxically reduce total investment and group welfare. Asymmetry leads to a disproportionate burden on those at greatest risk, making the system more vulnerable to shocks. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence for how efficiency in asymmetric public goods games depends on player count, discount factor, and the structure of risk, but it does not address punishment or sanctioning. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is highly informative for baseline (control) efficiency in asymmetric, no-punishment settings, especially regarding the effects of group size and discounting. However, it does not inform the effect of enabling punishment, as no such mechanism is present or analyzed. Its findings suggest that efficiency can be non-monotonic in group size and is sensitive to asymmetry and time preferences, which should be considered when predicting control efficiency, but it cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment.

- source: Henrich, J., Chudek, M., & Boyd, R. (2015). The Big Man Mechanism: how prestige fosters cooperation and creates prosocial leaders. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that prestige-based leadership, via cultural transmission, can generate and sustain cooperation in public goods game environments, leading to higher group efficiency and payoffs. The effect depends on the probability that followers copy the leader (prestige effect, p), the stickiness of acquired behaviors (s), and group size (n). Cooperation is more likely to evolve and persist in small groups, with high prestige effects and high stickiness. The model predicts that prestigious leaders will be more prosocial in small groups, but this effect diminishes in large groups. The paper does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms in its models.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public goods games without punishment, the presence of prestige-biased cultural transmission and prosocial leaders can increase group efficiency, especially in small groups and when followers are likely to copy and retain the leader's cooperative behavior. However, because the models do not include punishment or sanctions, the findings do not directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The results are most relevant for understanding baseline efficiency in games with prestige-based leadership and social learning, not for predicting the marginal effect of punishment. If a game design includes prestige or leading-by-example mechanisms, this paper suggests that efficiency may already be elevated, and the incremental effect of punishment may be smaller or interact with these mechanisms.

- source: Killingback, T., Bieri, J., & Flatt, T. (2006). Evolution in group-structured populations can resolve the tragedy of the commons. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard public goods game with continuous investment, cooperation and high group efficiency do not evolve in well-mixed populations, but can evolve and be stably maintained in group-structured populations with limited dispersal. The mechanism relies on group structure and population dynamics, not on punishment, reputation, or optional participation. The model demonstrates that for a range of dispersal rates, high levels of cooperative investment (and thus high group payoffs) can be sustained. However, the model does not include any form of punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as punishment is explicitly excluded from the model. It is useful for understanding baseline efficiency in the absence of punishment and for considering the effects of group structure and dispersal, but it does not inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is relevant only as a control or baseline for environments without punishment.

- source: Takezawa, M., & Price, M. E. (2010). Revisiting The Evolution of Reciprocity in Sizable Groups: Continuous reciprocity in the repeated n-person prisoner's dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper shows that in repeated n-person public goods games, continuous reciprocity (where players match the average contribution of others) is much more robust than discrete reciprocity (all-or-nothing) in supporting the evolution of cooperation, especially as the marginal per capita return (mpcr) increases. The basin of attraction for continuous reciprocity grows almost linearly with efficiency, allowing cooperation to invade from low initial frequencies when mpcr is high. Assortative interaction (preferential grouping of reciprocators) further enhances the viability of continuous reciprocity, even at lower efficiency. However, both forms of reciprocity struggle to invade when efficiency is low or group size is large, unless assortativity is present. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the efficiency of cooperation (mpcr) is a critical moderator of the success of reciprocity-based cooperation in public goods games. In the absence of punishment, high mpcr (approaching 1) is necessary for cooperation to be stable or to invade. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the mpcr dimension should be weighted heavily when estimating efficiency outcomes in control (no-punishment) games. However, because the paper does not model punishment or sanctions, it does not directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It does, however, highlight that in high-mpcr environments, cooperation can be stable even without punishment, while in low-mpcr environments, cooperation is unlikely to persist without additional mechanisms.

- source: Hilbe, C., Wu, B., Traulsen, A., & Nowak, M. A. (2015). Evolutionary performance of zero-determinant strategies in multiplayer games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that repeated interactions (direct reciprocity) can sustain cooperation and high efficiency in small groups, but not in large groups. As group size increases, the evolutionary dynamics favor defectors and extortioners, leading to a sharp drop in average group payoff. The results are robust across both restricted (ZD strategies only) and general (all memory-one strategies) strategy spaces. The paper provides explicit formulas for payoffs and demonstrates, both analytically and via simulation, that group size is a critical moderator of efficiency in repeated public goods games without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, increasing group size in repeated public goods games leads to lower efficiency, even when direct reciprocity is possible. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that the baseline efficiency in control games without punishment will be high for small groups but will decline as group size increases. The paper does not provide evidence about the effect of enabling punishment, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency change when punishment is enabled. However, it does highlight that, without additional mechanisms (like punishment), large groups are unlikely to sustain cooperation or high efficiency.

- source: Boza, G., & Számadó, S. (2010). Beneficial laggards: multilevel selection, cooperative polymorphism and division of labour in Threshold Public Good Games. *Bmc Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in threshold public good games, stable polymorphism (coexistence of cooperators and defectors) can be maintained by multilevel selection, even in the absence of punishment or spatial structure. Group efficiency (measured as group payoff or average fitness) is maximized when the number of cooperators matches the threshold required for success, not when all members cooperate. Increasing group size or cost of cooperation reduces the range of parameters where cooperation is stable. Division of labour can evolve, with individuals specializing in different collective actions. Spatial structure further promotes cooperation. The paper does not address punishment or its effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effects of punishment or sanctions in public good games. It is highly relevant for understanding baseline efficiency and the evolution of cooperation in threshold PGGs without punishment, and for predicting the efficiency of control games (punishment disabled) as a function of group size, cost, and threshold. However, it does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency when punishment is enabled, nor does it provide evidence on any moderators of punishment effects. It is useful for modeling baseline efficiency in threshold PGGs, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment.

- source: Hauert, C., Holmes, M., & Doebeli, M. (2006). Evolutionary games and population dynamics: maintenance of cooperation in public goods games. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games, if population density is allowed to vary as a function of average payoff, cooperation can be maintained through eco-evolutionary feedback. When defection leads to low payoffs, population density drops, which in turn reduces group size and can make cooperation advantageous. This can result in stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors at intermediate frequencies, with population density and average payoff stabilizing at levels where neither strategy dominates. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as punishment is not modeled. However, it is highly informative about how ecological feedback (variable group size as a function of payoff) can stabilize cooperation and improve group payoffs in the absence of punishment. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it provides a theoretical baseline for understanding efficiency in public goods games without punishment or reward.

- source: Zhang, Y. L., Wu, T., Chen, X. J., Xie, G. M., & Wang, L. (2013). Mixed strategy under generalized public goods games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary outcome in generalized public goods games with mixed strategies depends critically on the shape of the production function, the group size, the population size, and the cost-to-benefit ratio. For linear production functions, the outcome is determined by the cost/benefit ratio. For nonlinear functions, concave shapes favor stable coexistence (CESS), convex shapes favor bistability (repeller), and sigmoidal shapes can produce more complex dynamics with multiple equilibria. When the group size equals the population size, cooperation is always lost. The paper provides explicit conditions for the emergence of full cooperation, full defection, or intermediate equilibria, but does not address the effects of punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed theoretical predictions about how efficiency (group payoff relative to the cooperative optimum) in public goods games depends on the production function, group size, population size, and cost/benefit ratio, but it does not address punishment or sanctioning. Therefore, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. However, it is highly informative for understanding baseline efficiency in control games without punishment, especially as a function of game design parameters such as group size, population size, and the nonlinearity of the public goods production function.

- source: Olejarz, J. W., & Nowak, M. A. (2014). Evolution of staying together in the context of diffusible public goods. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in public goods games with diffusible goods can evolve if the benefit-to-cost ratio exceeds a threshold determined by the probability of staying together (q) and the internal absorption efficiency (alpha_k). Clustering increases the efficiency of cooperation by allowing cooperators to preferentially benefit from their own public goods. The model also shows that if defectors and cooperators differ in their ability to absorb public goods, coexistence or bistability can occur. Introducing a cost to large clusters (gamma_k) can create an optimal cluster size for cooperation. The model provides explicit analytical conditions for these outcomes, but does not consider punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how structural and spatial factors (such as clustering and internal absorption efficiency) affect the evolution of cooperation and group efficiency in public goods games. However, it does not address punishment or sanctioning, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. Its results are most relevant for predicting efficiency in control games without punishment, or in environments where clustering and spatial structure are the main mechanisms supporting cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how efficiency depends on clustering, internal benefit allocation, and the benefit-to-cost ratio, but offers no evidence about punishment effects.

- source: Salahshour, M. (2021). Freedom to choose between public resources promotes cooperation. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that allowing individuals to choose between multiple public goods games (resources) with different enhancement factors can promote cooperation. Defectors tend to cluster in the highest quality resource, while cooperators can 'shelter' in lower quality resources, avoiding exploitation. Cooperation is maximized when the two highest quality resources are similar in quality. The model is analyzed both analytically (replicator-mutator dynamics) and via simulation, showing robust results across parameter ranges. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present. The main outcome is the density of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is highly relevant for understanding how choice between public resources affects cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The model does not include punishment, reward, or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding the impact of resource choice and group structure on cooperation.

- source: Broom, M., Lafaye, C., Pattni, K., & Rychtár, J. (2015). A study of the dynamics of multi-player games on small networks using territorial interactions. *Journal of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the fixation probability of cooperators in the public goods game is generally less than 1/N (where N is the number of players), indicating that cooperation is disfavored under the birth-death process in these small networked populations. The fixation probability is strongly influenced by the mean temperature (a measure of how often individuals are replaced), with higher temperatures amplifying selection and lower temperatures suppressing it. The specific graph structure (network topology) has some effect, but mean temperature is a more important determinant. The results are derived for small graphs (3-4 players), and the framework is generalizable to larger graphs in principle. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are included in the models.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It does, however, provide theoretical insights into how network structure and group size affect the evolutionary success of cooperation versus defection in public goods games. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, but it may provide contextual background on how structural features (such as player count and group formation) influence cooperation in the absence of punishment.

- source: Wang, L. C., Liu, Y. Y., Guo, R. Q., Zhang, L., Liu, L. J., & Hua, S. J. (2024). The paradigm of tax-reward and tax-punishment strategies in the advancement of public resource management dynamics. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that both tax-funded punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation and sustain public resources, but only if the natural growth rate of the resource pool is sufficiently high. When the resource growth rate is low, even universal cooperation (driven by strong punishment or reward) cannot prevent resource depletion, so efficiency remains low. For moderate or high growth rates, the system can reach stable states with high cooperation and sustainable resources, but the tax-punishment model can exhibit bistability, meaning the final outcome depends on initial conditions. The magnitude of the tax (which determines the strength of punishment or reward) also affects whether the system stabilizes at full cooperation, coexistence, or defection. The models provide explicit, parameterized predictions for group efficiency and resource sustainability as a function of player count, tax size, and resource growth rate.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, parameterized theoretical predictions for the effect of punishment (and reward) on group efficiency and resource sustainability in public-goods-like resource management games. The models are not standard linear PGGs, but are close variants with dynamic, renewable resources and tax-based centralized punishment/reward. The findings show that the effect of punishment on efficiency is strongly moderated by the natural growth rate of the resource pool and the magnitude of the tax (punishment strength). For low growth rates, punishment does not improve efficiency; for moderate/high growth rates, punishment can improve efficiency, but outcomes may be bistable. The results are most applicable to settings where resource dynamics and centralized sanctioning are key features. The paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions in such environments.

- source: Okada, D., & Bingham, P. M. (2008). Human uniqueness-self-interest and social cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops a two-stage game-theoretic model in which players can cooperate or not, and, if there are non-cooperators, cooperators can choose to fight (coerce) or not. The key result is that the cost structure of coercion (punishment) is decisive: if coercion is cheap and effective (as in synchronous/remote attack, e.g., human projectile weapons), then robust equilibria with high cooperation and high efficiency are possible. If coercion is costly and ineffective (as in asynchronous attack, e.g., most non-human animals), only non-cooperation is robust, and group efficiency is low. The model thus predicts that enabling effective, low-cost punishment can transform a low-efficiency public goods game into a high-efficiency one, provided certain thresholds are met.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling effective, low-cost punishment (i.e., high punishment effectiveness per unit cost) in a public-goods-like environment can shift the equilibrium from low to high efficiency, especially as group size increases. The model is directly informative for the effect of punishment cost and punishment technology (effectiveness), and indirectly for player count and the structure of the contribution decision. It suggests that, all else equal, lowering punishment cost or increasing punishment effectiveness should make punishment more likely to increase efficiency. The findings are most relevant for one-shot or short games with simultaneous moves and no communication, and may be less directly applicable to repeated or highly structured lab experiments with additional features.

- source: Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2018). Punishment and inspection for governing the commons in a feedback-evolving game. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Punishment and inspection mechanisms can promote cooperation and prevent overexploitation in a common-pool resource game, but their ability to improve group efficiency (resource sustainability) depends critically on the intrinsic growth rate of the resource. If the resource renews too slowly, even perfect cooperation enforced by strong punishment leads to resource depletion and low efficiency. At moderate growth rates, strong punishment and inspection can achieve both high cooperation and sustainable resource levels, but weak institutions result in mixed strategy populations and depleted resources. At high growth rates, the resource is always sustainable regardless of punishment, and punishment only affects the fraction of cooperators versus defectors. Thus, the efficiency gain from punishment is context-dependent and can be absent or even negative if resource dynamics are unfavorable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like common-pool resource games is highly contingent on the ecological context, especially the resource's intrinsic growth rate. When predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, it is crucial to consider not only the game design dimensions (such as punishment cost and effectiveness) but also the underlying dynamics of the resource. In settings where the resource cannot recover quickly, punishment may not improve efficiency even if it increases cooperation. In more favorable ecological conditions, punishment can have a strong positive effect on efficiency, but only if it is sufficiently effective (high detection probability and/or fine). Thus, the paper suggests that the efficiency effect of punishment is not universal and must be interpreted in light of resource dynamics.

- source: Lee, J. H., Sigmund, K., Dieckmann, U., & Iwasa, Y. (2015). Games of corruption: How to suppress illegal logging. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that punishment (via hired enforcers) can sustain high efficiency (cooperation and group welfare) only if a sufficient fraction of enforcers are honest. If corruption is prevalent among enforcers, punishment fails and efficiency collapses. The system is bistable: both high- and low-efficiency equilibria are possible. Small rates of random exploration (mutation) among enforcers can tip the system toward either high or low efficiency, depending on whether honest or corrupt exploration is more common. Providing information about enforcer honesty (i.e., making corrupt enforcers identifiable) greatly increases the likelihood of high-efficiency outcomes. The model shows that interventions targeting enforcer honesty (education, incentives, transparency) are more effective for improving efficiency than interventions targeting harvesters directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in improving efficiency in public-goods-like games depends critically on the integrity of the punishment institution. If enforcers are honest and/or can be identified as such, punishment can lead to high efficiency. If enforcers are corrupt and cannot be identified, punishment may fail and efficiency may be low. The model suggests that game design dimensions that increase the likelihood of honest enforcement (e.g., transparency, information sharing, enforcer education) will increase the efficiency gain from punishment. The presence of bistability means that initial conditions and small biases (e.g., in enforcer behavior) can have large effects. For prediction, this paper implies that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the effectiveness and honesty of the punishment mechanism, and on whether players can identify corrupt enforcers.

- source: Okada, I., Yamamoto, H., Toriumi, F., & Sasaki, T. (2015). The Effect of Incentives and Meta-incentives on the Evolution of Cooperation. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a theoretical evolutionary game framework, cooperation and high efficiency (group payoff) can only be stably achieved if the incentive system includes second-order incentives for first-order rewards. Systems with only first-order punishment or reward, or with second-order incentives for punishment but not for reward, cannot maintain cooperation or efficiency in the long run due to the second-order free-rider problem. The cost-effect ratio of incentives (how much impact per unit cost) is a critical parameter: higher ratios make it easier for cooperation to be sustained. The model predicts that systems with second-order rewards for first-order incentives are more robust and can achieve high efficiency with fewer incentive-providers than systems with second-order punishments. The analysis is entirely theoretical, using replicator dynamics, and does not provide empirical estimates of efficiency but gives qualitative and parametric conditions for when efficiency is high or low under different incentive structures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment and reward systems (including meta-incentives) on efficiency in public-goods-like games. It suggests that simply enabling punishment is not sufficient for high efficiency; the structure of incentives, especially the presence of second-order incentives for rewarding, is crucial. If a game design includes only first-order punishment, efficiency will not improve in the long run. If second-order incentives for first-order rewards are present and the cost-effect ratio is high, efficiency can be high. The model also clarifies that the effect of punishment on efficiency is conditional on the broader incentive structure. For prediction tasks, this paper implies that the presence or absence of second-order incentives, and the cost-effectiveness of punishment/reward, are key moderators of the efficiency effect of punishment.

- source: Bowles, S., & Gintis, H. (2004). The evolution of strong reciprocity: cooperation in heterogeneous populations. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The model shows that in groups where some members are willing to incur costs to punish norm violators (strong reciprocators), high levels of cooperation and group efficiency are sustained. Without punishment, groups devolve to all-Selfish agents and low efficiency. The presence of punishment leads to a stable mix of Reciprocators, Cooperators, and Selfish agents, with low average shirking rates and high group payoffs. The model is robust to changes in group size, cost of punishment, and other parameters. The findings support the idea that costly punishment is a key mechanism for sustaining cooperation and efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling costly punishment in public-goods-game-like environments increases group efficiency and average payoffs, relative to control conditions without punishment. The model is not an empirical experiment but simulates a wide range of parameter values, showing that the positive effect of punishment on efficiency is robust to variations in group size, cost of punishment, and other design features. For prediction tasks, the presence of a costly punishment mechanism (even with moderate cost and effectiveness) should be expected to increase efficiency, especially in settings with repeated group interaction and no central authority. The model's structure and outcomes are directly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Lee, J. H., Jusup, M., & Iwasa, Y. (2017). Games of corruption in preventing the overuse of common-pool resources. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that delegated punishment (enforcement) can support efficient, fully cooperative outcomes in common-pool resource games, but only if a sufficient fraction of enforcers are honest. If corruption is prevalent among enforcers, punishment loses its effectiveness, and the system converges to defection and resource overuse (low efficiency). The model exhibits bistability: depending on initial conditions (notably the initial fraction of honest enforcers), the system ends up either in full cooperation (high efficiency) or full defection (low efficiency). Key parameters moderating the effect of punishment include group size (larger groups make cooperation harder), resource growth rate (higher growth rates favor defection), and the speed of resource dynamics (slower resource response masks the benefits of cooperation and impedes efficiency). The cost of harvesting has a mixed effect. The model provides direct predictions about how these dimensions affect the likelihood of efficient outcomes under punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like resource games depends critically on the honesty of enforcers and several game design dimensions. If punishment is implemented via delegated enforcement and enforcers are honest, punishment can enable high efficiency (full cooperation). However, if enforcers are corrupt, punishment fails and efficiency collapses. The model shows that increasing group size, increasing resource growth rate, and slowing resource dynamics all reduce the effectiveness of punishment in achieving efficiency. These findings should inform predictions about treatment efficiency: punishment will only improve efficiency if the enforcement mechanism is robust to corruption and the group size and ecological parameters are favorable. The model is most directly relevant to settings with delegated (not peer) punishment and where corruption is possible.

- source: Cooney, D. B. (2025). Exploring the Evolution of Altruistic Punishment with a PDE Model of Cultural Multilevel Selection. *Bulletin of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that altruistic punishment, when combined with group-level selection, can promote higher levels of cooperation and increase group efficiency compared to scenarios without punishment. However, the effect of punishment on efficiency is sensitive to the cost structure (per-interaction vs. fixed), the strength of punishment, and the form of group-level competition. In some models, increasing punishment strength or cost leads to higher cooperation but lower efficiency, due to the collective cost of punishment outweighing its benefits. The paper provides analytical expressions for the threshold group selection strength needed to sustain cooperation and for the steady-state average payoff, showing that these can depend non-monotonically on punishment parameters. The results highlight that the design of punishment mechanisms and the structure of group competition are critical moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, mechanistic, and quantitative guidance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments with group structure. It shows that the efficiency impact of punishment depends on the cost and strength of punishment, the type of cost (per-interaction or fixed), and the form of group-level competition. For prediction, the results suggest that: (1) punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when costs are per-interaction rather than fixed; (2) increasing punishment strength generally increases cooperation, but may decrease efficiency if costs are high; (3) the effect of punishment on efficiency can be non-monotonic, especially under group-level competition rules that normalize by maximum/minimum group payoffs; (4) the threshold group selection strength required for cooperation is lower with stronger or cheaper punishment. The paper's analytical formulas and numerical results can be used to estimate treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game design parameters, especially in settings with group-structured populations and multilevel selection.

- source: Weibull, J. W., & Salomonsson, M. (2006). Natural selection and social preferences. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary stability and efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-like games depend on the structure of reproductive externalities. When group members' fitness is positively affected by others' payoffs (cooperative breeding), punishment and cooperation can become stable and lead to higher group welfare, especially if the punishment is sufficiently harsh and the cost is not too high. In contrast, under strong reproductive competition, punishment can be stable but may not increase group efficiency. The model shows that the presence and effectiveness of punishment as a mechanism for promoting cooperation and efficiency is highly sensitive to the underlying fitness function and game parameters. The analysis provides explicit conditions for when punishment leads to more efficient, cooperative outcomes and when it does not.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on when punishment is likely to increase efficiency in public-goods-like games, depending on the game design and the nature of social/reproductive externalities. It suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not universal: it can be positive, nil, or even negative, depending on whether the environment favors cooperative or competitive externalities. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that game design dimensions such as the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the structure of payoffs, and the degree of group interdependence (e.g., MPCR analogs) are critical moderators. The findings support the idea that punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when group welfare is a significant component of individual fitness, and less likely (or even counterproductive) when individual competition dominates.

- source: POLLOCK, G. B. (1988). POPULATION-STRUCTURE, SPITE, AND THE ITERATED PRISONERS-DILEMMA. *American Journal of Physical Anthropology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that punishment-based strategies (TFT/NTFT) can maintain high group efficiency and suppress defection when the population is structured into multiple groups or dyads, allowing intergroup competition. The effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation and high payoffs is reduced as the scope of spite (the number of groups a defector can affect) increases or as population structure becomes less partitioned. In the limit of a single group (no structure), punishment cannot prevent defection and efficiency collapses. The analysis generalizes to n-person games, showing that the same logic applies: partitioned populations with group-level competition allow punishment to sustain cooperation and high efficiency, while unstructured populations do not.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-game-like environments will increase efficiency (group payoff) relative to control, but only when the game design includes sufficient population structure (multiple groups, limited overlap in group membership, or mechanisms for intergroup competition). If the game is a single group or highly overlapping, punishment will not improve efficiency. The findings are directly relevant for settings where the marginal per-capita return, group size, and population structure can be specified, and they highlight the importance of these dimensions as moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency.

- source: Kendal, J., Feldman, M. W., & Aoki, K. (2006). Cultural coevolution of norm adoption and enforcement when punishers are rewarded or non-punishers are punished. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both reward-based and punishment-based metanorms can enable the stable coexistence and fixation of norm-adopting punishers, leading to high group efficiency (mean fitness) under a wide range of parameter values. The presence of a metanorm (either rewarding punishers or punishing non-punishers) is necessary for this outcome; without it, the only stable equilibrium is full defection (no norm adoption, no punishment). The Reward Model is generally more effective than the Punishment Model in enabling the population to reach the efficient equilibrium, especially when starting from low frequencies of norm-adopters and punishers. The probability of fixation of the efficient equilibrium is higher under the Reward Model, and the region of attraction is larger. The models also show that cultural drift can facilitate the transition from the inefficient to the efficient equilibrium, especially in small populations and when costs of norm observation and punishment are low. The results are robust across a range of parameter values and are relevant to public goods game-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment (especially when reinforced by a metanorm, such as rewarding punishers or punishing non-punishers) can shift a population from an inefficient equilibrium (low efficiency, no cooperation) to an efficient one (high efficiency, full cooperation and sanctioning), provided the costs and benefits are favorable. The results suggest that, in public goods game-like environments, the introduction of punishment (with or without additional metanorms) can substantially increase group efficiency, especially if the cost of punishing is not too high and the benefit to punishers is sufficient. The Reward Model (rewarding punishers) is particularly effective, suggesting that institutional or social mechanisms that reward sanctioning may be more robust in promoting efficiency than those that only punish non-punishers. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, given knowledge of the cost/benefit structure and initial conditions. However, the results are theoretical and assume well-mixed populations and specific forms of cultural transmission.

- source: Powers, S. T., & Lehmann, L. (2013). The co-evolution of social institutions, demography, and large-scale human cooperation. *Ecology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the co-evolution of institutions with sanctioning (punishment) and demography enables the stable emergence and maintenance of large-scale cooperation, resulting in a dramatic increase in group carrying capacity and efficiency. In the absence of institutionalized punishment, cooperation cannot be stably maintained, and group size/efficiency remains low. The model demonstrates that the optimal institution invests most, but not all, of the public good into technological improvement (increasing carrying capacity), while retaining enough for sanctioning to deter defectors. Sensitivity analyses show that the positive effect of punishment on efficiency is robust to changes in key parameters, though it is diminished by high migration rates or low benefits of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling institutionalized punishment in public-goods-like environments will increase group efficiency, especially in structured populations. The model directly links the presence and effectiveness of punishment to higher group payoffs and larger sustainable group sizes. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, all else equal, introducing or strengthening punishment mechanisms should increase efficiency, provided the cost of punishment is not prohibitive and the benefit from cooperation is sufficient. The model also highlights the importance of population structure (limited migration) and the allocation of resources between sanctioning and productive investment. While the model is not an empirical experiment, its detailed mapping to public goods game structure and explicit focus on efficiency make it highly informative for predicting the impact of punishment on efficiency outcomes.

- source: Iwasa, Y., & Lee, J. H. (2013). Graduated punishment is efficient in resource management if people are heterogeneous. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that graduated punishment (punishment increasing with the harm caused by selfish actions) is only efficient—i.e., maximizes group welfare—when two conditions are met: (1) there is heterogeneity among players in their sensitivity to utility differences, and (2) there is a nonzero probability of false accusation (punishing prosocials by mistake). In standard rational or homogeneous quantal response models, optimal punishment is not graduated but rather jumps from zero to a high level. With heterogeneity and error, the optimal punishment increases smoothly with harm, matching the graduated punishment observed in successful resource management. The paper provides explicit formulas for optimal punishment and shows how efficiency-maximizing punishment depends on model parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that the efficiency impact of punishment in public-goods-like games depends critically on the presence of player heterogeneity and error in monitoring. In standard homogeneous or error-free settings, punishment is either absent or severe, with little efficiency gain from graduation. When heterogeneity and error are present, graduated punishment is optimal and can improve efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency will be more positive in environments with player heterogeneity and monitoring errors, and that the optimal punishment level should increase with the harm caused by defection. The paper's analytic results can inform parameterization of efficiency predictions as a function of harm, benefit, error rates, and heterogeneity.

- source: Lee, J. H., & Iwasa, Y. (2014). Modeling socio-economic aspects of ecosystem management and biodiversity conservation. *Population Ecology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_tech
  findings: The paper's third model demonstrates that in social dilemma situations (akin to public goods or common-pool resource games), introducing punishment for selfish behavior can increase group welfare, especially when punishment is graduated (i.e., increases with the harm caused). The model shows that with evaluation errors and heterogeneity in player sensitivity to utility, graduated punishment is optimal for maximizing efficiency (group payoff). Severe, non-graduated punishment is only optimal under unrealistic assumptions. The findings support the use of graduated sanctions to improve efficiency in resource management games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in public-goods-like environments with social dilemmas, enabling punishment (especially graduated punishment) increases group efficiency/welfare compared to no-punishment baselines, provided that punishment is not excessively severe and is sensitive to the harm caused. The model is abstract but maps closely to PGG-like games with two actions (cooperate/defect), and the efficiency outcome is directly analyzed. The findings are most applicable to settings where punishment can be graduated and where player heterogeneity and evaluation errors exist. The paper does not provide empirical effect sizes or parameterized predictions, but it gives qualitative guidance that enabling (graduated) punishment should increase efficiency relative to control.

- source: Nakamaru, M., & Iwasa, Y. (2006). The coevolution of altruism and punishment: Role of the selfish punisher. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment can promote the evolution of cooperation (and thus higher group payoffs) in both well-mixed and lattice-structured populations, but only under certain conditions. In the score-dependent viability model, punishment is more effective at suppressing defectors and promoting cooperation than in the fertility model. The presence of selfish punishers (defectors who punish other defectors) can help the evolution of altruistic punishers by first eliminating pure defectors, but pure altruists (who cooperate but do not punish) can hinder the evolution of punishment by being exploited. The benefit of cooperation, the fine imposed by punishment, and the cost of punishing all interact to determine evolutionary outcomes. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus context-dependent and can be positive or negative depending on parameter values and population structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency (group payoff) in public-goods-like games depends on the interaction of several design dimensions: population structure (well-mixed vs. lattice), the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the benefit of cooperation, and the evolutionary update rule (viability vs. fertility). Punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when the punishment fine is large relative to the cost of punishing, and in spatially structured populations. However, if the cost of punishing is too high or if pure cooperators are common, punishment may not evolve or may even reduce cooperation. The findings suggest that predictions of treatment efficiency from control efficiency should account for these moderators, especially the structure of interactions and the punishment parameters. However, as the paper does not report explicit efficiency values, its support is indirect and based on evolutionary stability of cooperation.

- source: Nakamaru, M., & Dieckmann, U. (2009). Runaway selection for cooperation and strict-and-severe punishment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in spatially structured populations, the co-evolution of cooperation and punishment (with continuous strategies for both) can lead to runaway selection for both high cooperation and strict-and-severe punishment. This dynamic is most pronounced when punishment is not too costly and cooperation is neither too cheap nor too expensive. The model shows that strict punishment norms (high strictness and severity) evolve naturally and reinforce high cooperation, resulting in much higher group-level cooperation than in the absence of punishment. In contrast, in well-mixed populations, punishment and cooperation do not evolve. The findings suggest that punishment can greatly enhance group cooperation and, by implication, group efficiency, but only under certain cost structures and spatial conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in spatially structured public-goods-like environments can substantially increase group cooperation and, by extension, efficiency—especially when punishment is strict, severe, and not too costly. The model suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the cost structure of cooperation and punishment, and on the population structure (spatial vs. well-mixed). For prediction tasks, the findings imply that punishment will have a more positive effect on efficiency when: (1) the game is spatially structured (analogous to local interactions), (2) punishment is strict and severe, (3) costs of punishment and cooperation are not too high, and (4) baseline cooperation is low without punishment. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide direct empirical efficiency ratios, so its quantitative predictions should be interpreted with caution.

- source: Currie, T. E., Campenni, M., Flitton, A., Njagi, T., Ontiri, E., Perret, C., & Walker, L. (2021). The cultural evolution and ecology of institutions. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
