It finds that punishment and norm enforcement are present in many societies, but their prevalence, forms, and social acceptance vary widely. Socio-ecological factors (community size, pathogen prevalence, subsistence type) and cultural factors (individualism/collectivism, honor, kinship intensity, power distance, trust) are discussed as moderators of punishment behaviors. While some included studies use public goods games with punishment, the review does not aggregate or report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral and attitudinal measures of punishment and norm enforcement.

However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. For the downstream prediction task, this review may help identify potentially relevant moderators (e.g., community size, cultural values) but does not supply parameter-level evidence or efficiency outcomes needed for direct prediction. - source: Syi (2014). Hidden patterns of reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper formalizes and compares four second-order action rules for direct reciprocity (e.g., Tit-for-tat, Contrite Tit-for-tat, Pavlov, Grim Trigger) and four second-order assessment rules for indirect reciprocity (e.g., Image Scoring, Simple Standing, Stern Judging, Shunning). It shows that these rules can be paired and exhibit similar patterns, especially in their relationship to punishment. The analysis highlights that moderate punishment policies (e.g., Simple Standing, Stern Judging) are most successful at maintaining cooperation, while extreme policies (e.g., Shunning, Grim Trigger) can undermine cooperation. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions or empirical evidence about efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and mechanistic insight into how different punishment and reciprocity rules may affect cooperation in repeated social dilemmas, but it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants. Its value for the prediction task is limited to theoretical framing: it suggests that moderate, justified punishment rules are more likely to sustain cooperation (and thus potentially higher efficiency), while overly harsh or overly lenient rules may be less effective. However, these insights are not tied to specific game design dimensions or empirical efficiency data.

- source: Hooper, P. L., Kaplan, H. S., & Jaeggi, A. V. (2021). Gains to cooperation drive the evolution of egalitarianism. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The model demonstrates that punishment of dominance (hawkishness) by withholding cooperation (defection in the PD game) can be evolutionarily stable and lead to egalitarian outcomes when the future benefits of cooperation outweigh the short-term gains from dominance. The efficiency of the group increases as leveller strategies proliferate, reducing the frequency of costly contests and increasing resource sharing. Simulations show that an ecological shift increasing the gains to cooperation leads to a transition from dominance/defection equilibria to egalitarian/cooperative equilibria with higher payoffs. Empirical analysis of forager societies finds that greater reliance on cooperative hunting predicts a higher probability of egalitarianism, consistent with the model's predictions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment (here, the ability to withhold cooperation from dominant individuals) increases group efficiency and resource equality, especially when the marginal benefit of cooperation is high and the probability of future interaction is high. The model is adjacent to public goods games but not an exact match; however, the logic and payoff structure are highly relevant. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that in repeated, high-mpcr, high-future-interaction environments, enabling punishment (even in the form of exclusion or shunning) will increase efficiency relative to control. The effect is stronger when the benefits of cooperation are high relative to the benefits of dominance or defection.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that only a small subset of reputation assignment rules—those that treat defection against bad individuals as good (i.e., as a form of justified punishment or sanction)—can sustain high efficiency (group payoff close to the maximum possible) in indirect reciprocity games. These 'leading eight' rules enable stable cooperation by allowing individuals to refuse help to bad actors without losing their own good reputation. The model shows that, under these rules, the average payoff in the population approaches the maximum possible (b-c), minus a small term due to errors. The results are robust to error rates and to the benefit-to-cost ratio, as long as b > c. The paper provides a formal evolutionary justification for the importance of sanctioning/punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency in reputation-based social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms—specifically, the ability to sanction or refuse help to bad actors without reputational cost—are critical for sustaining high efficiency in indirect reciprocity environments. While the model is not a standard public goods game, it is closely related: it is a repeated, pairwise helping game with a social dilemma structure. The findings suggest that, in environments where individuals can sanction defectors (and where such sanctioning is not itself punished), group efficiency can approach the maximum possible. For prediction tasks, this implies that enabling punishment or justified sanctioning in a game design is likely to increase efficiency, provided the reputation system is designed so that justified punishment does not harm the punisher's reputation. The results are robust to errors and to variations in the benefit-to-cost ratio, as long as cooperation is individually costly but socially beneficial.

- source: Nakamura, M., & Ohtsuki, H. (2014). Indirect reciprocity in three types of social dilemmas. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, reward_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in two-player simultaneous-move social dilemmas, the stability and efficiency of cooperation via indirect reciprocity depend on the structure of the social norm (reputation updating rule) and the underlying game. In Snowdrift and Prisoner's Dilemma, strong punishment of defectors (assigning bad reputations for defection against good players) is necessary to maintain high efficiency (i.e., stable cooperation with high group payoff). In Stag Hunt, such punishment is less critical, and norms that unfairly favor reciprocators (always assign good reputation to reciprocation) can enhance the robustness and efficiency of cooperation. The paper also shows that punishing unconditional cooperators (who help bad players) is important for maintaining efficient cooperation in all three games. The efficiency outcome (i.e., group payoff relative to full cooperation) is maximized under certain social norms, but the effect of punishment-like mechanisms is context-dependent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment (via reputation loss) on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends on the underlying game structure and the details of the punishment mechanism (social norm). In games with strong temptation to defect (SG, PD), enabling punishment (bad reputation for defectors) increases efficiency by stabilizing cooperation. In coordination games (SH), such punishment is less necessary, and norms that favor reciprocators can be more effective. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency should be moderated by the type of social dilemma and the specific implementation of the punishment/reputation mechanism. The results are most directly relevant to two-player games, not standard multi-player PGGs, but the logic may transfer to small-group settings with reputation-based punishment.

- source: Quiñones, A. E., van Doorn, G. S., Pen, I., Weissing, F. J., & Taborsky, M. (2016). Negotiation and appeasement can be more effective drivers of sociality than kin selection. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: Negotiation and appeasement mechanisms, modeled as responsive strategies where dominants can threaten aggression (punishment) and subordinates can appease by helping, lead to higher group productivity than kin-selected (unconditional) helping. The model shows that when negotiation is possible, even unrelated individuals can achieve high levels of help and group output, with low realized aggression. High relatedness, however, reduces the effectiveness of punishment/enforcement, leading to lower help and lower group productivity. Thus, punishment-like negotiation mechanisms are more effective at driving efficient cooperation than kin selection alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence of responsive punishment or enforcement mechanisms (negotiation) increases group efficiency in public-goods-like environments, compared to settings where only kin selection operates. The model is not a literal PGG, but the structure is closely analogous: individuals can help (contribute) or not, dominants can punish (aggression), and group output (productivity) is tracked. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment/negotiation will increase efficiency, especially when relatedness is low. The effect is robust across a range of parameter values. For prediction tasks, this paper supports a positive effect of punishment-enabled conditions on efficiency, and highlights that high relatedness may reduce the marginal benefit of punishment.

- source: Castro, L., Serrano, J. M., & Toro, M. A. (1998). Conceptual capacity to categorize and the evolution of altruism. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the Assessor strategy, which incorporates a punishment-like mechanism (reprobation) based on observed or reported selfish behavior, achieves higher fitness (i.e., greater group efficiency or payoff) than standard reciprocal strategies like Tit-for-Tat or Observer Tit-for-Tat when competing against always-defect strategies. This advantage is robust across both unstructured and family-structured populations, provided certain thresholds of cooperation frequency are met. The Assessor strategy is especially effective when individuals can observe or be informed about others' reprobation of selfish behavior, allowing them to withhold cooperation from defectors more efficiently. The model predicts that such punishment-like mechanisms can support higher levels of cooperation and group payoff than reciprocity alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment or sanctioning mechanisms (here, reprobation) in repeated social dilemma games can increase group efficiency/payoff relative to control conditions without such mechanisms. The findings are most directly relevant to iterated prisoner's dilemma settings, which are adjacent to public goods games, and suggest that the ability to observe and act on others' punishment signals is a key moderator. For prediction tasks, the results imply that, all else equal, introducing a punishment mechanism should be expected to increase efficiency, especially when players can observe or learn about others' punishment actions. However, the results are theoretical and do not provide quantitative effect sizes or empirical validation.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Iwasa, Y. (2006). The leading eight: Social norms that can maintain cooperation by indirect reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity games with binary reputations, only eight specific social norms (the 'leading eight') can maintain both evolutionary stability and a high level of cooperation/efficiency, even when errors in action and assessment occur. These norms require players to cooperate with good-reputation individuals, punish defectors, justify punishment, and allow for apology and forgiveness. Under these norms, the average payoff per interaction is close to the theoretical maximum (b-c), and the fraction of cooperative (good) players remains high, with only a small reduction due to errors. The paper provides analytical conditions for when these norms are stable and efficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in indirect reciprocity games with binary reputations and one-shot interactions, the structure of the social norm (reputation dynamics) is critical for achieving high efficiency. Specifically, norms that combine justified punishment (refusing to help bad actors and not being penalized for it) and forgiveness (allowing recovery from bad reputation) can maintain efficiency close to the maximum. While the game is not a standard public goods game, the findings are highly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms in repeated or reputation-based social dilemmas. The results suggest that, when punishment is structured as justified refusal to cooperate (and not as costly peer punishment), and when forgiveness is possible, efficiency losses due to punishment can be minimized or avoided. The paper does not address group size, communication, or other PGG-specific dimensions directly, but it does show that the design of sanctioning and reputation mechanisms is a key moderator of efficiency.

- source: Johnstone, R. A., & Bshary, R. (2008). Mutualism, market effects and partner control. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the threat of terminating an interaction (partner control) can suppress cheating and maintain cooperation, but its effectiveness depends on the relative abundance of exploiters and victims (market effects). When victims are rare, they can enforce cooperation and achieve higher payoffs; when they are common, exploiters can cheat more, reducing the efficiency of the interaction. If exploiters gain more control over the duration of interaction, they can extract more benefit, potentially driving the system into parasitism (negative payoffs for the victim). The model provides explicit conditions for when mutualism (positive payoffs) or parasitism (negative payoffs) will occur, and shows that efficiency is maximized when partner control is strong and the cost of finding new partners is high for exploiters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment-like partner control on efficiency in public-goods-like environments is highly sensitive to the structure of partner choice and market conditions. If the 'punishment' (exit threat) is credible and the cost of finding new partners is high for exploiters, efficiency is increased. If exploiters can easily find new victims, or if they gain control over the interaction, efficiency can decrease or even become negative for the exploited party. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency will depend on parameters analogous to partner abundance, search costs, and control over interaction duration. The model does not map directly onto standard PGGs, but its logic is relevant for settings where partner choice or exit is possible.

- source: Gintis, H. (2003). The hitchhiker's guide to altruism: Gene-culture coevolution, and the internalization of norms. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that internalized prosocial norms, including altruistic punishment, can become evolutionarily stable through gene-culture coevolution, especially when the fitness benefits of prosocial norms are high, extraparental socialization is strong, and assortative mating is present. Simulations show that groups with internalized altruistic punishment achieve high mean fitness and eliminate antisocial norms. Altruistic punishment is particularly effective at sustaining cooperation and group efficiency because its cost is lower and more concentrated than the cost of direct cooperation. The models predict that, in the presence of punishment, group efficiency and mean fitness are higher than in its absence, especially in large groups or when repeated interaction is infrequent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-like environments increases group efficiency and mean payoff, especially when direct cooperation is not stable due to high costs. The models suggest that punishment is a robust mechanism for sustaining cooperation and high group payoff, even in large groups or with high migration, as long as the cost of punishment is low relative to the benefit of cooperation. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where internalized norms and cultural transmission play a role, and less so to short-run laboratory games, but the core logic supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like dilemmas.

- source: Furuzono, Y., Yamakawa, T., Ohnishi, S., & Kaneko, T. (2013). Combined effects of social pressure from the inside and outside of fishing communities promote cooperation for stock management. *Ecological Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops a coupled socioeconomic-bioeconomic model of a fishing community, where individuals choose to cooperate with management or not, and non-cooperators experience psychological punishment ('social pressure') that depends on both internal (community-based) and external (outside community) sources. The model shows that when both internal and external social pressures are sufficiently strong, the system can reach full cooperation, resulting in sustainable fish stocks and maximum sustainable yield (MSY)—a proxy for group efficiency. If only one type of social pressure is strong, or both are weak, the system may stabilize at lower cooperation and lower stock levels. The model highlights the importance of both community relationships and external societal pressure in promoting cooperation for resource management.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that psychological punishment mechanisms (social pressure) can increase group efficiency in common-pool resource settings analogous to public goods games, especially when both internal and external sources are present. While the model is not a standard PGG and does not report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff, it shows that the presence and strength of punishment-like mechanisms (social pressure) are critical moderators of group outcomes. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling both internal and external punishment/sanction mechanisms is likely to increase efficiency, while the absence or weakness of either reduces the effect. The model also suggests that the effect of punishment is moderated by group size (player count), as social pressure per individual decreases with larger groups.

- source: Lehmann, L., & Keller, L. (2006). The evolution of cooperation and altruism - a general framework and a classification of models. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation and altruism can only evolve when at least one of four general conditions is met: (1) direct benefits to the actor, (2) repeated interactions with information about partner behavior, (3) preferential interactions with kin, or (4) genetic linkage between recognition and helping traits (greenbeard effect). Punishment, coercion, and policing are highlighted as mechanisms that can shift the cost-benefit ratio in favor of cooperation, making it more likely to evolve and persist. The paper reviews and classifies a large number of theoretical models, showing that all can be mapped onto these four categories. It emphasizes that punishment is effective in promoting cooperation when it increases the cost of defection relative to cooperation, but notes that the stability of punishment depends on its own cost and on whether it is linked to cooperation genetically or behaviorally.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms can increase the efficiency (fitness, group payoff, or productivity) of cooperative systems by making defection less attractive. It does not provide quantitative predictions for specific game designs, but it clarifies that the effectiveness of punishment depends on its ability to alter the cost-benefit ratio and on the presence of at least one of the four general conditions for cooperation. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that punishment will generally increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments if it is not prohibitively costly and if it is implemented in a way that aligns with one of the four evolutionary pathways. However, the paper does not address specific design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, chat) in the context of laboratory or field experiments, so its guidance is primarily mechanistic and qualitative.

- source: Safarzynska, K. (2013). The coevolution of culture and environment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that costly resource conservation (including punishment of defectors) can evolve in structured populations facing renewable resource constraints and environmental shocks. Groups with many defectors are more likely to overharvest and collapse, while groups with more cooperators and punishers are more resilient. Punishment reduces the within-group advantage of defection, but is itself costly. Environmental shocks (weather) accelerate the elimination of non-cooperative groups. Between-group interactions (resource-conflict and harvest-sharing) modulate the evolution of cooperation: resource-conflict increases the frequency of resource conservation by increasing collapse of defector-heavy groups, while harvest-sharing reduces group collapse and allows more defection. Larger group size has a non-monotonic effect: above a threshold, larger groups are more likely to collapse, which can favor the evolution of conservation. Policies that increase the cost of extraction or penalize defectors promote conservation. However, the model does not report group efficiency or total payoff as a primary outcome, focusing instead on behavioral strategy frequencies and group survival.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that punishment and resource conservation can evolve and persist in group-structured resource dilemmas, especially under environmental variability and when group collapse is possible. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff in public goods games or their close variants. The model's outcomes are mainly about the frequency of cooperative/punishing strategies and group survival, not efficiency as defined for the prediction task. Thus, while the mechanisms discussed (e.g., punishment reducing defection, group collapse selecting for cooperation) are relevant for understanding when punishment might improve group outcomes, the paper does not provide data or results that can be directly mapped to efficiency predictions for specific game design dimensions.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & Bshary, R. (2011). Resolving the iterated prisoner's dilemma: theory and reality. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that while the strict assumptions of the IPD are rarely met in nature, the pay-off structure (temptation to cheat, mutual investment) is relevant to many real-world cooperative interactions. Punishment (negative reciprocity) is identified as a mechanism that can sustain cooperation in IPD-like games, as illustrated by the cleaner fish example, where dominant individuals punish defectors to promote future cooperation. However, the paper does not report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral strategies and the conditions under which cooperation is sustained.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that punishment can promote cooperation in iterated social dilemmas, especially when cooperation is not all-or-nothing and when partner choice or punishment is possible. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it systematically vary game design dimensions. As such, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, but it does support the plausibility of punishment as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation in IPD-like settings.

- source: Boehm, C. (1997). Impact of the human egalitarian syndrome on Darwinian selection mechanics. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proposes that in prehistoric human forager societies, egalitarian social norms—enforced by moralistic punishment and social sanctions—dampened within-group competition and amplified between-group variation. This, in turn, empowered group selection and enabled the evolution and maintenance of altruistic and cooperative behaviors, despite the presence of free riders. The author argues that active punishment and social isolation of noncooperators reduced the free-rider problem, making group-beneficial traits more likely to persist. However, these arguments are theoretical and based on ethnographic and evolutionary reasoning, not on experimental or quantitative data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment and social sanctions can promote cooperation and suppress free riding in group contexts, which is relevant to public goods game environments. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it specify how game design dimensions (such as punishment cost, player count, or MPCR) would moderate these effects. Its value for prediction is limited to supporting the plausibility of punishment as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation, not for estimating efficiency outcomes in specific game designs.

- source: Riehl, C., & Frederickson, M. E. (2016). Cheating and punishment in cooperative animal societies. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that in animal societies, cheating (adaptive non-cooperation that increases the cheater's fitness at the expense of others) is rare, and when it does occur, punishment is also rare or not clearly evolved as a response to cheating. Most uncooperative individuals have lower, not higher, fitness than cooperators, and variation in cooperation is often due to condition or context rather than strategic cheating. Where punishment or policing is observed (e.g., worker policing in social insects, aggression toward non-helping subordinates), it is often not clearly linked to increased group efficiency or evolved specifically to suppress cheating. The review concludes that cooperation is usually stable because it is directly or indirectly beneficial, not because punishment enforces it.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, especially in the context of game design dimensions. It suggests that, in natural animal societies, punishment is not a robust or general mechanism for increasing group efficiency or suppressing cheating, and that cooperation is often stable for other reasons. For prediction tasks, this implies that the presence of punishment mechanisms may not reliably increase efficiency unless cheating is truly adaptive and punishment is both targeted and effective. The review also highlights the importance of context, such as group size, relatedness, and the direct benefits of cooperation, which may moderate the effect of punishment.

- source: Chalub, F. A. C. C., Santos, F. C., & Pacheco, J. M. (2006). The evolution of norms. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that, in a two-level selection model with reputation-based indirect reciprocity, simple and unambiguous social norms emerge that maximize group efficiency (average payoff). The dominant norm (10011001) enforces cooperation by rewarding help to good individuals and punishing refusal to help good individuals or helping bad individuals. This norm allows for forgiveness and rapid recovery of reputation, but also implacable punishment for bad actions. Under this norm, cooperative strategies (CO, OR) dominate, and islands reach maximal possible fitness. The model demonstrates that the evolution of such norms is robust across a wide range of benefit parameters (b), and that a small amount of migration between islands is essential for the evolution of efficient norms and strategies. The findings support the idea that reputation-based punishment and norm enforcement can sustain high efficiency in group-structured populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that reputation-based punishment (loss of reputation for non-cooperation) in repeated social dilemma games can lead to the evolution of norms that maximize group efficiency. The model is not a standard public goods game, but rather a give-and-receive game with binary actions and reputation updating. However, the core mechanism—punishment via reputation loss, indirect reciprocity, and norm enforcement—closely parallels the logic of punishment in public goods games. The findings suggest that, when such mechanisms are present, enabling punishment (via reputation or direct sanctions) is likely to increase group efficiency relative to control conditions without punishment. The results are robust across a range of benefit-to-cost ratios and do not depend on fine-tuning of parameters. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that introducing effective punishment or norm enforcement in repeated social dilemma environments will increase efficiency, especially when group structure and reputation are salient.

- source: Inaba, M., Takahashi, N., & Ohtsuki, H. (2016). Robustness of linkage strategy that leads to large-scale cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that a linkage strategy—where defectors in a social dilemma are excluded from indirect reciprocity benefits—can be evolutionarily stable and maintain high group efficiency, but only when there are implementation errors in the social dilemma and no perception errors. In the absence of errors, linkage and non-linkage strategies are neutral, allowing non-linkage strategies to drift in and eventually enable defectors to invade, collapsing cooperation and efficiency. Perception errors (misjudging others' actions) undermine the stability and efficiency of linkage strategies, as they disrupt the ability to assortatively cooperate and exclude defectors. The results highlight that the effectiveness of exclusionary sanctions (linkage) for maintaining efficiency is highly sensitive to the type and prevalence of errors in the system.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment-like exclusion mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that exclusionary sanctions (linkage) can increase efficiency, but only if players can reliably observe and agree on others' actions (i.e., low perception error) and if there is some noise in implementation (so that linkage strategies can distinguish themselves from non-linkage cooperators). If perception errors are common, the efficiency benefit of punishment/exclusion is undermined. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is implemented as exclusion from future benefits rather than direct costly punishment, and where the information environment is well-specified. The results are less directly transferable to standard PGGs with explicit costly punishment, but the mechanisms and moderators (error types, information) are highly relevant for prediction.

- source: Fishman, M. A. (2006). Involuntary defection and the evolutionary origins of empathy. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated dyadic cooperation games with involuntary defection, unconditional punishment (retaliation for any defection) undermines cooperation and reduces efficiency, as it leads to cycles of mutual defection. In contrast, an empathic retaliator strategy—where punishment is withheld if the opponent's defection follows one's own involuntary defection—can be evolutionarily stable and support high efficiency, especially when the expected number of repeated interactions is large. The stability and efficiency of cooperation depend on the cost of helping, the probability of being able to help, and the expected number of interaction cycles. The paper provides explicit conditions and payoff matrices for these outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the design of punishment mechanisms critically affects efficiency in repeated cooperation games. Specifically, unconditional punishment (retaliation) in the presence of involuntary defection leads to lower efficiency, while empathic (conditional) punishment can sustain high efficiency if repeated interactions are frequent. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends on whether punishment is unconditional or conditional/empathic, the frequency of repeated interactions, and the possibility of involuntary defection. However, the model is not a public goods game, and punishment is not costly in the sense of standard PGG experiments; rather, it is refusal to help. Thus, direct quantitative predictions for PGGs should be made with caution, but the qualitative insight is that punishment can either harm or help efficiency depending on its design and the game context.

- source: Kurokawa, S. (2022). Evolution of trustfulness in the case where resources for cooperation are sometimes absent. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma with stochastic absence of resources for cooperation, the evolutionarily stable outcome is often a coexistence of trustful cooperators and trustful defectors, provided the cost-to-benefit ratio, the probability of lacking resources, and the exogenous break-up rate are not too high. Trustful strategies (those that keep interacting with partners who defect due to lack of resources) are robustly favored by natural selection, and this leads to higher equilibrium frequencies of cooperation and higher group payoffs (efficiency). When parameters are unfavorable (high cost, high resource absence, high break-up), defectors dominate and efficiency is low. The model does not include explicit punishment or costly sanctioning, but the 'walk-away' (partner switching) mechanism is functionally adjacent to punishment in that it can enforce cooperation by breaking ties with defectors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not model a public goods game or explicit punishment, it analyzes a closely related repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with partner choice (walk-away) and stochastic resource constraints. The key design dimensions mapped are the cost-to-benefit ratio (analogous to MPCR), the probability of resource absence (a form of noise or cooperation constraint), and the exogenous break-up rate (analogous to the probability of game continuation or group stability). The main result is that mechanisms allowing players to break ties with defectors (even when defection is due to lack of resources) can support high efficiency, provided the game is not too harsh (low cost, low noise, stable groups). This suggests that in PGG-like environments, enabling partner choice or walk-away (an adjacent form of sanction) can increase efficiency, especially when the baseline efficiency is not too low. However, the absence of explicit punishment means the results are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled PGGs, but the logic of positive assortment and the conditions for efficiency gains are highly relevant.

- source: Ghachem, M. (2016). The institution as a blunt instrument: Cooperation through imperfect observability. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that institutional tagging, even when imperfect, can substantially increase the efficiency (payoff) of cooperation in social dilemmas like the Prisoner's Dilemma. The key mechanism is that tagged cooperators are more likely to be chosen for interaction, leading to non-uniform interaction rates that favor cooperators. The model provides analytical conditions under which cooperation is favored by selection, is risk dominant, or is more abundant in the mutation-selection equilibrium. The positive effect on efficiency is robust to moderate costs of tag production and search. The effect is highly sensitive to the institution's error rates, especially the rate at which defectors are mistakenly tagged as cooperators. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, but institutional tagging serves a similar function by enabling partner selection based on observed tags.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that institutional mechanisms which increase the observability of cooperation (even imperfectly) can increase group efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. While not a public goods game per se, the findings are adjacent and likely transferable to PGG-like settings where partner choice or reputation is possible. The model suggests that, in environments where institutions can credibly tag cooperators and individuals can use these tags to select partners, enabling such mechanisms will increase efficiency relative to a control with no tagging. The effect is stronger when the institution is more accurate (especially in avoiding false positives for defectors), when more of the population is screened, and when individuals can search more for partners. The results are less directly applicable to settings where punishment is the main intervention, but the underlying logic (institutional information enabling discrimination) is relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment-like or reputation-based interventions.

- source: Kraines, D. P., & Kraines, V. Y. (2000). Natural selection of memory-one strategies for the iterated prisoner's dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in the iterated Prisoner's Dilemma with memory-one strategies and evolutionary dynamics, the population tends to evolve toward a small set of stable strategies. The Pavlov (Win-Stay Lose-Switch) strategy is particularly robust, achieving high average payoffs and being resistant to invasion by other strategies, especially in the presence of noise. The analysis partitions the space of strategies into basins of attraction: cooperative (high payoff), defecting (low payoff), alternating (zero payoff), and a small equilibrium plane. The size of these basins depends on the initial conditions and the payoff matrix. The paper also shows that strategies that combine trust with readiness to retaliate (but not excessive forgiveness) are most successful. While explicit punishment mechanisms are not modeled, the evolutionary success of strategies that retaliate after being exploited is highlighted.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not study public goods games or explicit punishment institutions, it analyzes the efficiency (average payoff) of strategies in a closely related repeated social dilemma (the IPD) under evolutionary dynamics. The findings suggest that strategies incorporating contingent retaliation (analogous to punishment) can sustain high efficiency, but only if they avoid excessive forgiveness or spite. The mapping of strategy space to efficiency outcomes can inform predictions about the likely efficiency of repeated games with different behavioral rules, especially regarding the role of contingent sanctions. However, the lack of explicit group structure, multi-player interactions, and institutional punishment limits direct transfer to public goods games.

- source: Masuda, N. (2012). Ingroup favoritism and intergroup cooperation under indirect reciprocity based on group reputation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of cooperation in group-structured indirect reciprocity games depends critically on the social norm used for reputation assignment and the frequency of intragroup versus intergroup interactions. Full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency, with payoff b-c per round) is stable only when all observers use a 'serious' social norm (standing or judging) for all types of interactions. If observers use double standards (different norms for ingroup and outgroup interactions), then ingroup favoritism emerges, leading to lower efficiency (payoff reduced by the fraction of intergroup interactions). The key condition for any cooperation to be stable is b*r_in > c, where r_in is the probability of intragroup interaction. The model shows that changing the social norm (e.g., forcing consistency across interaction types) can shift the equilibrium from inefficient ingroup favoritism to efficient full cooperation. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward, but reputation loss/gain serves a similar function.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting efficiency in indirect reciprocity games with group structure. It shows that the efficiency outcome (full cooperation, partial, or ingroup favoritism) is determined by the social norm for reputation assignment and the structure of interactions (r_in). In the absence of explicit punishment or reward, but with reputation-based indirect reciprocity, efficiency is maximized only if the social norm is consistent and 'serious' (standing/judging) across all interaction types. If the game design allows or encourages double standards (different norms for ingroup/outgroup), expect lower efficiency due to ingroup favoritism. The model's efficiency results are directly parameterized by the benefit-to-cost ratio and the frequency of intragroup interaction, which can be mapped to MPCR and group structure in PGG-like games. However, the absence of explicit punishment/reward means the results are most applicable to games where reputation is the main enforcement mechanism.

- source: Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2023). Grouping promotes both partnership and rivalry with long memory in direct reciprocity. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The main finding is that group structure, when combined with the availability of longer-memory strategies (which allow for friendly rival strategies), leads to the evolution of nearly full cooperation and maximal efficiency in the repeated donation game. In well-mixed populations, longer memory does not have this effect. The results are robust to the benefit-to-cost ratio and to details of the evolutionary process. The paper does not study public goods games or explicit punishment mechanisms, but the repeated donation game is adjacent to PGGs, and friendly rival strategies function as a form of endogenous sanctioning (never allowing the co-player to earn more). Efficiency is measured as the fraction of time mutual cooperation is achieved, which is equivalent to group payoff relative to the maximum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in two-player repeated donation games (adjacent to PGGs), the combination of group structure and the availability of longer-memory strategies (enabling friendly rival strategies) can dramatically increase efficiency, even when cooperation would otherwise be difficult. While there is no explicit punishment mechanism, friendly rival strategies act as endogenous enforcers of cooperation by never allowing the co-player to earn more. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in environments with group structure and sufficient strategic complexity, efficiency can be much higher than in well-mixed or memory-limited settings, even without explicit punishment. However, the results may not transfer directly to multi-player PGGs or to settings with explicit costly punishment.

- source: Kayser, K., & Lampert, A. (2021). Survivor's dilemma: The evolution of cooperation in volatile environments. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in the survivor's dilemma, cooperation can be evolutionarily stable if the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and/or environmental volatility is low. In these cases, mutual cooperation leads to higher expected lifespan (efficiency) for all. However, if the benefit-to-cost ratio is low or volatility is high, defection dominates and efficiency is lower. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, indirect punishment occurs because defectors lose partners and thus reduce their own survival prospects. The findings are presented for both 2- and 3-player games, with detailed analysis of parameter regions where cooperation or defection is stable. The results are directly about efficiency (expected lifespan) and its dependence on game parameters, but do not address the effect of enabling or disabling explicit punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence about how efficiency (expected lifespan) in repeated social dilemmas depends on group size, benefit-to-cost ratio, and environmental volatility. It does not directly address the effect of enabling or disabling punishment, as no explicit punishment mechanism is present. However, it does show that indirect forms of 'punishment' (loss of cooperative partners) can sustain cooperation and efficiency under certain conditions. For prediction tasks involving explicit punishment in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that efficiency gains from cooperation depend strongly on the benefit-to-cost ratio and the likelihood of future interaction (volatility), and that even without explicit punishment, cooperation can sometimes be stable. The model is adjacent to PGGs and does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a treatment.

- source: Johnstone, R. A., & Bshary, R. (2007). Indirect reciprocity in asymmetric interactions: when apparent altruism facilitates profitable exploitation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in asymmetric games where only one party (the actor) can help or exploit, and the other (the recipient) can only choose whether to interact, a form of indirect reciprocity can evolve. Actors help when the gains from exploitation are low to maintain a positive image, which encourages recipients to approach them in the future, allowing profitable exploitation when the gains are high. This strategy is stable and increases the long-term average payoff for both actors and recipients, provided certain conditions on observability, state-switching, and payoff parameters are met. The model shows that efficiency (average group payoff) is higher under these conditions than if actors always exploit or if recipients always avoid interaction.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in asymmetric games with image scoring and conditional approach, mechanisms analogous to punishment (here, avoidance of exploiters) can increase efficiency by supporting conditional cooperation. The findings suggest that when harmful behavior is observable and actors' states vary, efficiency is maximized for intermediate values of key parameters (cost of harm, switching probability, observability). While not a PGG or direct punishment setting, the model is adjacent and supports the idea that social mechanisms that deter exploitation (even without direct punishment) can improve group efficiency. For prediction, the paper suggests that enabling mechanisms for reputation-based avoidance or indirect sanctions can increase efficiency, especially when exploitation is not always profitable and can be detected.

- source: Johnstone, R. A., & Bshary, R. (2002). From parasitism to mutualism: partner control in asymmetric interactions. *Ecology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The model demonstrates that in asymmetric interactions, the ability of the exploited party to terminate the interaction acts as a deterrent to exploitation, increasing the efficiency (joint payoff) of the system. As the exploiter's power to prolong the interaction increases, exploitation rises, the victim's payoff falls (eventually becoming negative), and the system shifts from mutualism to parasitism. The temptation to cheat (benefit/cost ratio) and the rate at which mutualistic benefits diminish also modulate these outcomes. The model is supported qualitatively by empirical data from cleaner-fish systems, where larger clients (with more to gain from cleaning) experience less exploitation and longer interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that mechanisms allowing the exploited party to control the duration of interaction (analogous to exit, partner choice, or punishment in PGG-like games) can increase efficiency by deterring exploitation. While not a PGG or direct punishment experiment, the model is relevant for predicting that increasing the ability of group members to sanction or exit (or otherwise control exploiters) will generally increase group efficiency, especially when the temptation to cheat is not overwhelming. The model also suggests that efficiency gains from punishment or partner control are greatest when the benefits of cooperation are high and the costs of cheating are high relative to the exploiter's gain. However, the model does not address repeated group interactions, explicit punishment costs, or the full range of PGG design dimensions, so its predictive value is indirect.

- source: Peña, J., Heifetz, A., & Nöldeke, G. (2024). The shirker's dilemma and the prospect of cooperation in large groups. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in the shirker's dilemma, as group size increases, the proportion of volunteers at the stable equilibrium increases (for low enough costs), but the expected payoff (efficiency) decreases. The probability that the public good is provided can either increase or decrease with group size, depending on the cost of cooperation. In the limit of very large groups and low costs, the proportion of volunteers approaches one, and the expected payoff and success probability converge to positive values. However, the basin of attraction for the cooperative equilibrium shrinks with group size, making cooperation less likely to be reached from random initial conditions. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in a binary-action threshold collective action game (shirker's dilemma), increasing group size generally reduces efficiency (expected payoff at equilibrium), even though the proportion of volunteers may increase. The findings suggest that, in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, larger groups are less efficient, and the probability of successful collective action may not improve with group size. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that, in similar threshold public goods environments without punishment, efficiency will decrease as group size increases. However, because the model does not include punishment, it does not directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, but it does provide a theoretical baseline for control (no-punishment) conditions in threshold games.

- source: Kroumi, D. (2025). The role of selective extinction in promoting cooperation in a many demes model. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that structuring a population into demes and introducing selective extinction—whereby groups with more defectors are more likely to go extinct—can dramatically increase the evolutionary success (fixation probability) of cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma. Without selective extinction (\alpha=0), defection is always favored, and cooperation cannot persist. As the selective extinction parameter \alpha increases, the fixation probability of cooperation increases, and that of defection decreases. There are explicit threshold values of \alpha (functions of game payoffs and demographic parameters) above which cooperation is favored by selection, and above which it is more likely to fix than defection. The model also shows how the baseline extinction rate (v) and the structure of payoffs (T-R, P-S) modulate these thresholds. The results are robust to several alternative life-cycle assumptions. The main outcome is that group-level demographic feedback (selective extinction) can overcome the individual-level advantage of defection and promote group-level efficiency (cooperation).
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in structured populations with group-level demographic feedback (selective extinction of defect-dominated groups), the efficiency of the system (measured by the fixation probability of cooperation and thus long-run group payoff) can be substantially increased by increasing the strength of selective extinction (\alpha). In the absence of such feedback, efficiency remains low (defection dominates). The model gives explicit formulas for the critical value of \alpha needed to shift the system from defection-dominated to cooperation-dominated, as a function of game payoffs and demographic parameters (deme size, number of demes, baseline extinction rate). While the model is not a direct public goods game and does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, the selective extinction process is functionally analogous to a group-level sanction. Thus, the findings support the prediction that introducing or strengthening group-level sanctions (even if not individual punishment) can increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, especially when the control (no-sanction) efficiency is low.

- source: Garay, J., Csiszár, V., & Móri, T. F. (2014). Under multilevel selection: When shall you be neither spiteful nor envious?. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in structured populations with multilevel selection, the evolutionary stability of strategies depends on the relative strength of group-level survival effects versus within-group competition. When group survival (due to predation or intergroup conflict) is highly sensitive to group size (i.e., total fecundity), strategies that maximize group fecundity (neutral or donator) are favored, leading to higher group efficiency. When group-level effects are weak, spiteful or envious strategies can dominate, reducing group efficiency. The model shows that increasing the benefit of donation or the cost of spite/envious behavior further promotes efficient, cooperative strategies. The results are robust to parameter changes and apply to both predation and intergroup conflict scenarios.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in environments where group-level survival or payoff is highly sensitive to group size or total contribution, mechanisms that increase group efficiency (such as donation or cooperation) will be favored over spiteful or envious (punitive) strategies. While the model does not directly implement a public goods game with explicit punishment, it is adjacent: 'spiteful' and 'envious' strategies are analogous to costly punishment, and 'donator' is analogous to cooperation or reward. The findings suggest that increasing the importance of group-level outcomes (e.g., by increasing the marginal per-capita return or the impact of group payoff on survival) will make punishment less stable and cooperation more stable, thus increasing efficiency. However, the model is not a direct PGG and does not include explicit punishment technology or cost parameters as in standard PGG experiments.

- source: Sasaki, T., Okada, I., & Nakai, Y. (2016). Indirect reciprocity can overcome free-rider problems on costly moral assessment. *Biology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity models with costly assessment, cooperation is not stable unless there is a mechanism to sanction or exclude those who evade the cost of assessment. By introducing a pre-assessment stage that labels non-payers as 'nasty' and excludes them from receiving help, the model shows that a cooperative equilibrium (where everyone pays for assessment and helps others) can be locally stable if the benefit of helping exceeds the cost of assessment. This mechanism is analogous to pool punishment in public goods games and is effective under leading social norms. The findings are theoretical but directly address efficiency and the conditions for stable cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that institutional mechanisms to sanction or exclude cost-evaders (such as pre-assessment or pool punishment) can significantly improve efficiency in social dilemma games with costly monitoring or punishment. For prediction tasks, the presence of a pre-assessment or institutionalized punishment mechanism is likely to shift efficiency from low (collapse of cooperation) to high (stable cooperation), provided the net benefit of cooperation is positive. The results are robust across different social norms and suggest that the design of the punishment/sanctioning mechanism is a key moderator of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Fletcher, J. A., & Zwick, M. (2006). Unifying the theories of inclusive fitness and reciprocal altruism. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper demonstrates that the evolution of cooperation (altruism) in both kin and non-kin contexts can be predicted by a generalized Hamilton's rule that incorporates the covariance between genotypes and the cooperative behaviors of others, as well as nonadditive (synergistic) effects. In iterated prisoner's dilemma models, conditional strategies like tit-for-tat can increase in frequency when the benefit received from others' cooperation exceeds the cost, as predicted by Queller's rule. The same logic applies to mutualistic interactions between species. The paper shows that synergy (nonadditivity) and conditional behavior can both facilitate the evolution of cooperation, even in the absence of kinship or repeated interactions. Punishment is discussed as a possible mechanism for positive assortment but is not modeled. The findings are theoretical and do not provide empirical estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical background for understanding how mechanisms like punishment, synergy, and conditional behavior can affect the evolution of cooperation and group efficiency, but it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It suggests that any mechanism (including punishment) that increases positive assortment or synergy can increase the likelihood that cooperation evolves, but does not specify the magnitude or direction of efficiency changes in experimental settings. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper is useful for mechanistic context but not for direct quantitative prediction.

- source: Golubski, A. J., & Klausmeier, C. A. (2010). Control in mutualisms Combined implications of partner choice and bargaining roles. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the ability to appropriate surplus (analogous to control over punishment/reward) and the ability to choose partners (partner choice) interact to determine the evolution of mutualism and the distribution of payoffs in mutualistic associations. When the guild with partner choice also receives the surplus, mutualistic strategies are favored and average payoffs are high. However, the presence of empty slots or high frequencies of exploiters can lead to cyclical dynamics where exploitative strategies temporarily increase, reducing average payoffs. The model predicts that partner choice is most effective at selecting against moderately exploitative partners, but highly exploitative partners can persist under some conditions. The overall effect on efficiency is mixed: partner choice and surplus control can increase efficiency, but not always, especially if exploiters are present or if association opportunities are limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that mechanisms analogous to punishment (partner choice, surplus appropriation) can increase efficiency (group payoff) in mutualism-like bargaining games, especially when combined. However, the effect is not universally positive: the presence of exploiters and the structure of association opportunities (e.g., empty slots) can lead to reduced efficiency or cyclical dynamics. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling partner choice and surplus control generally increases efficiency, but the effect may be moderated or reversed if exploiters can persist or if association opportunities are limited. The model is adjacent to public goods games, so its predictions should be transferred with caution, but it provides informative indirect evidence about the conditions under which punishment-like mechanisms improve or fail to improve efficiency.

- source: Wang, R. W., & Shi, L. (2010). The evolution of cooperation in asymmetric systems. *Science China-life Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in asymmetric cooperative systems, the net benefit (efficiency) to the recipient depends critically on the intrinsic growth rates of benefit and cost, the strength of density-dependent interference competition among cooperative actors, and the availability of common resources. Stronger density-dependent interference competition among cooperative actors and higher initial relatedness (intrinsic contribution ratio) increase the probability of maintaining cooperation (positive efficiency). However, changes in these parameters or resource saturation can lead to a transition from cooperation to conflict, with net benefit becoming negative. The model also shows that recipient-enforced discrimination (punishing cheaters, rewarding cooperators) can increase initial relatedness and favor cooperation, but does not guarantee global stability. The system can exhibit oscillatory or chaotic dynamics between cooperation and conflict.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how efficiency in public-goods-like asymmetric systems is affected by the number of actors, resource saturation, and density-dependent competition. It suggests that efficiency gains from cooperation are not guaranteed by punishment or sanctions alone, but depend on ecological and structural parameters such as the strength of interference competition and resource availability. For prediction tasks, the model implies that enabling punishment (in the form of recipient-enforced discrimination) can increase efficiency only when it effectively increases initial relatedness and when density-dependent competition among actors is strong enough to prevent resource overexploitation. However, efficiency can decrease or oscillate if these conditions are not met. The findings are most relevant for systems with asymmetric roles and may be less directly transferable to standard symmetric PGG lab games.

- source: Lotem, A., Fishman, M. A., & Stone, L. (2003). From reciprocity to unconditional altruism through signalling benefits. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated cooperation games with individual variation in quality, the addition of signalling benefits to helping can lead to stable unconditional altruism among high-quality individuals. This occurs when the signalling benefit (S) is greater than the cost of helping (C) for high-quality individuals but not for low-quality individuals. Under these conditions, high-quality individuals cooperate unconditionally, while low-quality individuals defect or play tit-for-tat. The result is a higher average payoff (efficiency) for high-quality cooperators, and the overall level of cooperation and efficiency in the population is increased compared to models without signalling benefits. The model also shows that if signalling benefits become too cheap (affordable to all), the correlation between quality and cooperation disappears, undermining the signalling function.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that adding signalling benefits to cooperation games can increase efficiency, especially among high-quality individuals. For prediction tasks, it suggests that in environments where helping can serve as a costly signal (and where the signalling benefit exceeds the cost for some but not all), enabling such signalling will increase the efficiency of cooperation relative to control. The model is adjacent to public goods games and punishment, but the payoff logic and ESS analysis are directly relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes when game design allows for signalling or reputation effects. However, the model does not directly manipulate punishment or public goods game parameters, so its predictive value is strongest for settings where signalling is a plausible mechanism.

- source: Pacheco, J. M., Santos, F. C., & Chalub, F. A. C. C. (2006). Stern-judging: A simple, successful norm which promotes cooperation under indirect reciprocity. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that, in a multilevel evolutionary model of indirect reciprocity, the stern-judging norm—where helping good individuals or refusing help to bad individuals is rewarded, and the opposite is punished—emerges as the dominant and most robust social norm. This norm leads to maximized average payoffs for groups, indicating high efficiency in sustaining cooperation. The result is robust to different forms of group selection and inter-tribe conflict. The model suggests that simple, reputation-based punishment mechanisms are sufficient to promote and maintain high levels of cooperation and group welfare, even in the presence of errors and migration.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that simple, reputation-based punishment mechanisms (like stern-judging) can robustly increase group efficiency and cooperation in indirect reciprocity settings. While the model is not a public goods game per se, it is adjacent: it involves costly helping, reputation, and punishment via social norms. The findings suggest that, in environments where indirect reciprocity and reputation are salient, enabling punishment (in the form of social norm enforcement) is likely to increase group efficiency relative to no-punishment baselines. However, the model is abstract and does not manipulate most game design dimensions directly, so its predictive value is strongest for settings with similar indirect reciprocity structure and less for standard PGGs.

- source: Morsky, B., Plotkin, J. B., & Akcay, E. (2024). Indirect reciprocity with Bayesian reasoning and biases. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that Bayesian reasoning about errors in action and observation generally reduces the equilibrium level of cooperation and group payoff in indirect reciprocity games, compared to models where agents do not reason about errors. This effect is robust across most social norms (Shunning, Simple Standing, Staying, Stern Judging), but under the Scoring norm, Bayesian reasoning can actually promote cooperation and support a mixed equilibrium with all strategy types. The impact of Bayesian reasoning is strongest when error rates are low. Optimism bias (overestimating the frequency of good individuals) generally undermines cooperation, while mild pessimism bias can sometimes promote it, but strong biases of either type destroy cooperation. The model provides heatmaps and bifurcation diagrams showing how cooperation and payoffs change with error rates and biases. The findings are relevant for predicting how changes in information processing, error rates, and social norms affect group efficiency in reputation-based cooperation games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting efficiency in public-goods-like games with punishment/reward mechanisms based on reputation. It shows that the cognitive sophistication of agents (Bayesian reasoning about errors) can reduce group efficiency, except under certain social norms (Scoring). The results suggest that, in environments where punishment/reward is reputation-based and subject to errors, enabling more sophisticated reasoning may not always increase efficiency. The effect of punishment (via conditional defection) on efficiency is highly sensitive to the social norm, error rates, and the presence of optimism/pessimism bias. For downstream prediction, the paper supports the view that punishment mechanisms relying on reputation can backfire or be less effective if agents reason about errors, unless the norm is simple (Scoring). The findings are most transferable to settings where punishment is implemented as conditional cooperation/defection based on reputation, rather than direct costly punishment.

- source: Graser, C., Fujiwara-Greve, T., García, J., & van Veelen, M. (2025). Repeated games with partner choice. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
