  findings: The paper finds that introducing the option to leave one's partner in repeated prisoner's dilemma games (i.e., partner choice) leads to a substantial increase in average cooperation rates across a wide range of benefit-to-cost ratios and continuation probabilities. This is because partner choice enables endogenous assortment: cooperators can avoid defectors by leaving, which stabilizes cooperation. Theoretical analysis and agent-based simulations both show that, with partner choice, the population spends more time in cooperative states and escapes from universal defection more quickly. The paper compares strategies that punish by defecting (grim trigger) to those that punish by leaving, and finds that punishing by leaving is more robust to invasion and more likely to support cooperation. Although the main outcome is cooperation rate, higher cooperation implies higher average payoffs, so efficiency is indirectly improved by partner choice.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that enabling partner choice (the ability to leave and rematch) in repeated prisoner's dilemma games increases cooperation and, by implication, group efficiency/payoff. While the setting is not a public goods game per se, the mechanism is closely related, and the results are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments where partner choice is possible. The paper does not study costly punishment or explicit sanctions, but rather 'punishment' by leaving. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling partner choice should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a control with no partner choice, especially in repeated dyadic games. The results are most directly relevant when the game is dyadic, repeated, and partner choice is the main intervention.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., Iwasa, Y., & Nowak, M. A. (2015). Reputation Effects in Public and Private Interactions. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the stability of cooperative strategies in indirect reciprocity depends critically on the observability of private actions (q), the frequency of public interactions (p), and the benefit-to-cost ratio (b/c). Honest cooperation (leading to full group efficiency) is only stable when private actions are sufficiently observable and b/c is high. When private actions are rarely observed or b/c is low, only partial cooperation or defection is stable, resulting in lower group efficiency. The model provides explicit analytical thresholds for these transitions. The presence of public observation increases the incentive for cooperation, but if private actions are unobserved, individuals may defect in private, reducing overall efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how observability (analogous to information feedback or monitoring in public goods games) and the benefit-to-cost ratio moderate the effect of reputation-based incentives on group efficiency. While not a direct PGG or punishment study, its results suggest that increasing the probability that actions are observed (or reputationally consequential) increases the likelihood of stable, high-efficiency cooperation. In prediction tasks, similar mechanisms (e.g., transparency, monitoring, or reputation updating) should be expected to increase the efficiency gains from reputation or sanctioning systems, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high. However, if observability is low, the efficiency impact of such systems will be limited.

- source: Brandt, H., & Sigmund, K. (2004). The logic of reprobation: assessment and action rules for indirect reciprocation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The study shows that indirect reciprocity models with assessment rules that distinguish justified from unjustified defections (STANDING) are more effective at sustaining cooperation and suppressing defectors than simple scoring rules, especially in the presence of errors. However, even simple scoring can support cooperation if the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and players have enough interactions. The model demonstrates that the structure of the population, the number of observed interactions, and the error rates in action and perception all influence the stability of cooperation. Analytical results show that, under certain assumptions about the distribution of the number of rounds, stable mixtures of discriminating and non-discriminating altruists can exist, leading to high average payoffs. The findings suggest that costly punishment (refusing to help) can sustain cooperation, but is less robust than non-costly forms (STANDING).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment-like mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation (and thus efficiency) depends on whether punishment is costly, the benefit-to-cost ratio, error rates, and the number of interactions. While the model is not a standard PGG, the parallels are strong: refusing to help is analogous to punishing defectors, and the group payoff is analogous to efficiency. The results imply that costly punishment can increase efficiency, but is less robust than non-costly punishment, and that the details of the assessment and action rules, as well as the game structure (e.g., number of rounds, observation probability), are important moderators. For prediction, the paper supports the view that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and errors are not too frequent, but the effect is sensitive to the costliness and implementation of punishment.

- source: Aktipis, C. A. (2004). Know when to walk away: contingent movement and the evolution of cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that a simple contingent movement rule ('Walk Away') enables cooperation to evolve and persist in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments, outperforming more cognitively complex strategies like TFT and PAVLOV under many conditions. Walk Away is robust to invasion, can invade populations of defectors at lower initial frequencies than other strategies, and is most successful when movement costs, error rates, and agent density are low. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and outcomes are measured by the evolutionary success of strategies (frequency in the population), not by direct group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that contingent movement (exit) can substitute for more complex memory-based or punishment-based strategies in promoting cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It does not study public goods games or explicit punishment, but shows that contingent movement (exit after defection) can promote cooperation and evolutionary success in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments. The findings suggest that mechanisms allowing agents to avoid defectors (analogous to exit or partner choice) can be highly effective, even without explicit punishment or memory. However, because the model does not report group efficiency or total payoff, and does not manipulate punishment cost or technology, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency in public goods games with punishment is limited. The results are most relevant for understanding the role of partner choice, mobility, and spatial structure as alternatives or complements to punishment.

- source: Romano, A., Saral, A. S., & De Dreu, C. K. W. (2025). Reciprocity evolves more readily in competitive than cooperative socio-ecologies. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: Reciprocal matching strategies, which reward cooperation and punish defection, are more likely to evolve and persist in competitive socio-ecologies where the risk of exploitation is high (e.g., Chicken/Hawk-Dove/Attack-Defend games) than in cooperative ones (Stag Hunt). In cooperative environments, unconditional cooperation dominates, while in competitive environments, anti-reciprocity (mismatching) and defection are more successful. The success of reciprocity increases with the probability of repeated interaction. The paper does not directly report on group efficiency or total payoff, but the evolutionary success of reciprocity implies it is adaptive in competitive settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the effectiveness of punishment (as part of reciprocal strategies) in promoting cooperation depends on the competitiveness of the environment and the risk of exploitation. While it does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes directly, it suggests that punishment (as part of reciprocity) is more likely to be adaptive and prevalent in competitive, high-risk environments. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of punishment on efficiency may be more positive in competitive, high-exploitation-risk games (e.g., Chicken, Hawk-Dove) than in cooperative games (e.g., Stag Hunt). However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Wu, J. H., Számadó, S., Barclay, P., Beersma, B., Cruz, T. D. D., Lo Iacono, S., Nieper, A. S., Peters, K., Przepiorka, W., Tiokhin, L., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2021). Honesty and dishonesty in gossip strategies: a fitness interdependence analysis. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the honesty of gossip is determined by the gossiper's fitness interdependence with the receiver and target, and by the marginal cost/benefit structure of the underlying game between receiver and target. A simple matching rule is proposed: gossipers are honest when their fitness interdependence aligns with the effect of honest gossip, and dishonest when it mismatches. The model is applied to four game types, including a punishment game, but the focus is on the strategic choice of gossip honesty rather than on direct efficiency or payoff outcomes. The paper suggests that honest gossip can support cooperation, but that dishonest gossip can undermine reputation-based cooperation, especially when fitness interdependence is negative or mixed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how information transmission (gossip) can act as a low-cost reputational punishment mechanism in social dilemmas, including punishment games. However, it does not provide empirical or simulated data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate or analyze the standard public goods game dimensions. Its main contribution is to clarify when gossip will be honest or dishonest, which can indirectly affect cooperation and efficiency, but the paper does not quantify these effects. For prediction tasks about punishment effects on efficiency in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by highlighting the importance of information accuracy and fitness interdependence in the effectiveness of reputational sanctions.

- source: Manapat, M. L., Rand, D. G., Pawlowitsch, C., & Nowak, M. A. (2012). Stochastic evolutionary dynamics resolve the Traveler's Dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in the Traveler's Dilemma, evolutionary dynamics favor higher (more cooperative) claims when the reward/penalty R is small relative to the maximum claim M, and lower (less cooperative) claims when R is large. The model reproduces the empirical finding that average claims decrease as R increases. The analysis is focused on the distribution of claims, not on group efficiency or total payoff, and the Traveler's Dilemma is structurally different from public goods games: it is a two-player, competitive undercutting game with a specific bonus/penalty mechanism, not a multi-player contribution game. The findings are relevant for understanding how incentive structures (like R) shape cooperative behavior, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or punishment effects in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, adjacent evidence for the prediction task. It shows that in a two-player social dilemma with a reward/penalty for undercutting (Traveler's Dilemma), increasing the size of the penalty/reward (R) leads to less cooperative behavior (lower claims). However, the game structure is not a public goods game, and the outcomes are not group efficiency or total payoff, but rather the average claim. There is no direct manipulation or analysis of punishment in a PGG or direct efficiency outcomes. Thus, the paper may inform general intuitions about how incentive structures affect cooperation, but should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Rankin, D. J., & Eggimann, F. (2009). The evolution of judgement bias in indirect reciprocity. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that judgement bias—weighting bad acts more heavily than good acts in reputation updating—can evolve in indirect reciprocity models, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is low. Under 'standing' reputation rules, negative judgement bias is favored over a wider range of parameters than under 'scoring.' Allowing judgement bias to evolve increases the level of cooperation in the population. The model does not include explicit costly punishment, but negative judgement bias acts as a form of social sanctioning by reducing the likelihood of future help to defectors. The model does not report group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it suggests that mechanisms that increase the reputational cost of defection (such as negative judgement bias) can promote cooperation, especially when the cost of cooperation is high. The findings are most relevant for settings where reputation-based indirect reciprocity is the main mechanism, rather than explicit costly punishment. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Yuan, H. R., Meng, X. Z., & Alzahrani, A. K. (2023). Analysis of dynamic evolution process of the N-player division of labor game model. *Mathematical Modelling of Natural Phenomena*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that high toxicity rates (especially against cheaters), high toxin levels, and smaller group sizes promote the disappearance of cheaters and the persistence of cooperation (cooperators and/or toxin producers). The system exhibits bistability: depending on initial conditions and parameters, the population may converge to all cooperators, coexistence of cooperators and toxin producers, or coexistence of cooperators and cheaters. The interior equilibrium (all three strategies coexisting) is always unstable. Bifurcation analysis reveals thresholds in absorption rates and toxin parameters that determine which strategies persist. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of each strategy at equilibrium, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how costly sanctioning (here, toxin production) can suppress free riding and promote cooperation in N-player games. However, the model is not a standard public goods game with explicit peer punishment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome. The findings suggest that increasing the effectiveness or prevalence of costly sanctions (analogous to punishment) can reduce free riding and increase the fraction of task-performers, especially in smaller groups and with higher sanction effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment-like mechanisms can increase cooperation, but does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes or direct mapping to standard PGG dimensions.

- source: Bruner, J. P., & Smead, R. (2022). Tag-based spite with correlated interactions. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of observable tags and the possibility of conditioning behavior on tags (e.g., only harming out-group members) fundamentally changes the evolutionary dynamics of costly harming (spite) in the Prisoner's Delight game. Tags make pure (unconditional) spite less likely to evolve even when anti-correlation would otherwise favor it, but make conditional (tag-based) spite more likely in some regimes. Several stable equilibria are identified, including cooperative polymorphisms (mostly cooperation, little spite), pure spite, and a novel 'treacherous polymorphism' where spite is directed within groups. The presence of tags generally reduces the prevalence of widespread spiteful behavior, even under conditions that would otherwise favor it. The results are framed in terms of evolutionary stability and behavioral frequencies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. The model is a two-player game (Prisoner's Delight) with costly harming, not a multi-player public goods game. The main outcomes are the evolutionary frequencies of behavioral types, not group efficiency or total payoff. However, the analysis is relevant for understanding how the structure of punishment-like mechanisms (costly harming, conditional on observable tags) and population structure (correlated/anti-correlated interactions) can affect the prevalence of cooperation and spite. The findings suggest that mechanisms allowing for conditional punishment (e.g., based on group identity) can both promote and suppress harmful behavior, depending on the context. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides indirect theoretical context about how punishment-like behaviors may interact with population structure and signaling, but does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Koike, S., Nakamaru, M., & Tsujimoto, M. (2010). Evolution of cooperation in rotating indivisible goods game. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a rotating indivisible goods game (a model of ROSCAs), cooperation collapses unless both a peer selection rule (based on reputation) and a forfeiture rule (excluding non-contributors from receiving goods) are present. Peer selection alone or forfeiture alone is insufficient. The model tracks the evolution of cooperation probabilities before and after receiving goods, showing that only the combination of both rules leads to sustained high cooperation. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it shows that in a public-goods-adjacent environment with indivisible goods, the presence of both peer selection (reputation-based group formation) and a forfeiture rule (exclusion of non-contributors) is necessary to sustain high cooperation rates. For prediction, this suggests that punishment-like mechanisms (here, exclusion and peer selection) may only be effective in combination, and that the structure of the game (indivisible goods, rotation, exclusion) is critical. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits its direct applicability to efficiency prediction.

- source: Lu, S. N., & Wang, Y. (2024). Adjustment of link weights based on tolerance promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Biosystems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to reduce interaction intensity with neighbors who defect beyond a tolerance threshold increases the frequency of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. However, if the tolerance threshold is too high (i.e., individuals are too forgiving), cooperation is undermined. The effect is stronger when more individuals in the population are 'environmental adaptors' who adjust their interaction intensity. The main outcome is the equilibrium frequency of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The findings are robust to changes in network size and initial cooperator fraction.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about mechanisms that may promote cooperation in repeated social dilemmas, specifically through adaptive partner selection or ostracism based on tolerance for defection. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, and the game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game. The results suggest that mechanisms allowing individuals to reduce interaction with defectors (after a tolerance threshold) can increase cooperation rates, but excessive tolerance undermines this effect. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not analyze punishment costs, efficiency, or public goods structures.

- source: Page, K. M., Nowak, M. A., & Sigmund, K. (2000). The spatial ultimatum game. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that spatial structure in the ultimatum game leads to the evolution of fairer offers and higher acceptance thresholds compared to non-spatial (random encounter) settings. In well-mixed populations, average offers and acceptance levels are low, approaching zero as mutation error decreases. In spatially structured populations (1D and 2D lattices), average offers and acceptance levels are much higher, approaching a fair split in some cases. The paper interprets rejection of low offers as a punishment mechanism that, when combined with spatial structure, supports the evolution of fairness. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to payoff-based prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that spatial structure and local competition can support the evolution of fairer, more cooperative behavior in bargaining games where punishment is endogenous (via rejection). However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of spatial structure and local interactions for the evolution of fairness and the effectiveness of endogenous punishment mechanisms.

- source: Wang, R. W., He, J. Z., Wang, Y. Q., Shi, L., & Li, Y. T. (2010). Asymmetric interaction will facilitate the evolution of cooperation. *Science China-life Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper develops a theoretical model of the asymmetric Hawk-Dove game, showing that the frequency of cooperation increases with greater asymmetry between players (i.e., when one player is much stronger or more dominant than the other). The model also shows that a lower cost-to-benefit ratio of cooperation increases cooperation frequency. The discussion links these findings to real-world systems where dominant individuals can punish non-cooperators, suggesting that credible punishment and asymmetry can stabilize cooperation. However, the paper does not provide direct predictions or data on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the probability of cooperation and conflict.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical support for the idea that asymmetry and credible punishment can increase cooperation rates in social dilemmas, but it does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff. Its relevance to predicting efficiency in public goods games with punishment is limited, as it analyzes a two-player Hawk-Dove game rather than a multi-player public goods game, and focuses on cooperation frequency rather than efficiency. The discussion of punishment is conceptual, not formalized as a game design dimension. Thus, while the paper suggests that punishment and asymmetry can facilitate cooperation, it does not provide direct evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Powers, S. T., van Schaik, C. P., & Lehmann, L. (2016). How institutions shaped the last major evolutionary transition to large-scale human societies. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proposes that the ability to create and enforce institutional rules, including sanctioning mechanisms, is a key driver of large-scale human cooperation and division of labor. Institutions can transform the default equilibrium of social dilemmas (such as public goods games) to more cooperative, higher-payoff outcomes by enabling coordination, information sharing, and self-enforcing sanctioning. Theoretical arguments and historical/anthropological examples are used to support the claim that institutions can increase group efficiency and welfare, but the paper does not provide new empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment or institutional design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual support for the idea that enabling punishment or sanctioning mechanisms within an institutional framework can increase efficiency and group payoffs in public-goods-like environments, especially as group size increases. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency changes as a function of game design dimensions or control efficiency. Its value for prediction is in supporting the plausibility and mechanism of positive effects of institutions (including punishment) on efficiency, but it does not inform the magnitude or specific moderators of these effects.

- source: Gardner, A., West, S. A., & Barton, N. H. (2007). The relation between multilocus population genetics and social evolution theory. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates that multilocus population genetics provides a general framework for modeling the evolution of social behaviors, including cooperation and punishment. In a simple two-locus model (one for cooperation, one for punishment), it finds that cooperation and punishment cannot invade unless cooperation is already favored by relatedness (i.e., punishment does not expand the parameter space for cooperation). Moreover, increased relatedness directly selects against punishment, as punishment is mutually costly. The only benefit to punishment comes from its association with cooperation, but this is insufficient to overcome the direct costs. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on evolutionary stability and gene frequency change.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding the evolutionary logic of cooperation and punishment, and suggest that costly punishment may not increase cooperation or group benefit except under restrictive conditions. However, because it does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it should not be used as a primary source for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in experimental public goods games. It may be useful for mechanistic or theoretical context, especially regarding the evolutionary logic of punishment and cooperation.

- source: Pandula, N., Akcay, E., & Morsky, B. (2024). Indirect reciprocity with abductive reasoning. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that abductive reasoning in indirect reciprocity models can, under certain social norms and low error rates, promote higher levels of cooperation than non-reasoning models. For the Scoring norm, abductive reasoning enables stable cooperative states that are not possible without reasoning. For other norms (Simple Standing, Staying, Stern Judging), abductive reasoning can outperform or underperform non-reasoning depending on the error rates and the degree of confirmation/falsification bias. Under the Shunning norm, abductive reasoning leads to only defection. The model is more robust to errors in observation/assessment than to errors in action. The main reported outcomes are the equilibrium frequencies of cooperative strategies and intended cooperation rates, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how reasoning about errors and reputation assignment can affect the evolution of cooperation in indirect reciprocity settings. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it suggests that the cognitive process for assigning reputations (abductive reasoning) and the social norm in use can be critical moderators of cooperation rates. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in PGG-like games with punishment, this paper is only adjacent: it does not model explicit PGGs, group-level payoffs, or direct costly punishment, but it does highlight the importance of error handling and norm choice for sustaining cooperation. Its findings may inform mechanistic priors about the robustness of cooperation to errors and the role of cognitive biases, but cannot be used for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Taylor, C., & Nowak, M. A. (2007). Transforming the dilemma. *Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper provides a unified framework for understanding how different mechanisms (direct reciprocity, indirect reciprocity, kin selection, group selection, network reciprocity) transform the payoff structure of the Prisoner's Dilemma to allow for the evolution of cooperation. For each mechanism, it derives the threshold conditions under which cooperation can be stable or invade. The analysis is mathematical and does not include empirical or simulation results. Punishment is discussed only as an extension, not as a primary focus, and is said to be subsumed under direct or indirect reciprocity. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment on these outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is a theoretical synthesis of mechanisms for cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma, with only a brief, conceptual discussion of punishment. The paper does not analyze or model the effect of punishment on group payoff, efficiency, or related outcomes, and does not provide parameterized results for game design dimensions relevant to the prediction task. Its main value is in clarifying that punishment is not a separate mechanism but an extension of reciprocity, and that efficiency or payoff outcomes are not directly addressed.

- source: West, S. A., Griffin, A. S., & Gardner, A. (2007). Social semantics: altruism, cooperation, mutualism, strong reciprocity and group selection. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that much confusion in the literature on cooperation, altruism, and punishment arises from inconsistent or ambiguous use of key terms. It clarifies that in many models (including public goods games), behaviors labeled as 'altruistic' or 'strong reciprocity' may actually be mutually beneficial, providing direct fitness benefits to the actor as well as to the group. Punishment, often called 'altruistic punishment,' can also be selfish or spiteful, depending on the context. The paper argues that clear definitions and careful distinction between proximate and ultimate explanations are essential for progress in the field. It also notes that the group selection and kin selection frameworks are mathematically equivalent in most cases relevant to cooperation and public goods games.

- source: André, J. B., & Morin, O. (2011). Questioning the cultural evolution of altruism. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that models of cultural evolution can, in principle, allow for the spread of biologically altruistic behaviors (including costly punishment) among nonkin, but only because of maladaptive features of social learning such as conformist or payoff-biased imitation. These mechanisms do not change the fundamental selective pressures on altruism; rather, they introduce constraints or errors that allow maladaptive behaviors to persist. The paper critiques the literature for sometimes conflating mutualistic cooperation (which can be adaptive) with genuine altruism (which is maladaptive by definition), and for overstating the explanatory power of cultural group selection. The authors argue that the prevalence of genuine altruism in human societies is likely limited and always reflects some form of maladaptation, whether cognitive or cultural.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence relevant to predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games or their variants. It does, however, offer theoretical caution: the presence of punishment or helping behaviors in cultural evolutionary models does not guarantee group-level efficiency or welfare gains, as these behaviors may be maladaptive and persist only due to constraints in learning or imitation. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the mere presence of punishment mechanisms (especially when costly) does not ensure improved efficiency, and that claims about the benefits of punishment should be scrutinized for underlying assumptions about adaptation and learning. However, the paper does not analyze or report efficiency, payoff, or welfare outcomes, so its direct value for quantitative prediction is limited.

It proposes that social norms and sanctions (including third-party punishment and internalized sanctions such as guilt and shame) emerged at the community level and were later internalized, giving rise to the feeling of moral obligation. The authors critique and build on the accounts of Tomasello and Darwall, emphasizing the group-level origins of normativity and the psychological mechanisms that support norm compliance. The paper does not address efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it present experimental or empirical evidence.

- source: Shimura, H., & Nakamaru, M. (2018). Large group size promotes the evolution of cooperation in the mutual-aid game. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in the mutual-aid game (an 'all for one' group helping structure), larger group size and more repeated interactions (rounds) both promote the evolution and stability of cooperation, as measured by the expected payoff of conditional cooperators relative to defectors. This is in contrast to the standard public goods game, where larger groups typically undermine cooperation. The effect is robust to implementation and perception errors, and is strongest under certain reputation assessment rules. The paper does not include punishment or costly sanctioning mechanisms; all incentives are mediated by reputation and conditional cooperation. The findings are directly about efficiency (payoff) outcomes, not just cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in mutual-aid games with indirect reciprocity and reputation-based conditional cooperation (but no explicit punishment), increasing group size and number of rounds both increase the likelihood that cooperation (and thus high efficiency) will evolve and persist. The results are not directly transferable to standard public goods games with punishment, but they suggest that the structure of the game (e.g., 'all for one' vs 'all for all') and the presence of reputation-based mechanisms can strongly moderate the effect of group size and repetition on efficiency. Since punishment is not present, the paper does not inform the effect of enabling punishment per se, but it does provide a baseline for efficiency in a related class of games without punishment.

  findings: The paper finds that costly signaling of cooperative intent can evolve as an honest signal when the long-term benefits of repeated cooperation outweigh the costs of signaling. In such environments, agents who signal are more likely to be chosen as partners, and those who are chosen are more likely to cooperate, leading to higher payoffs for both signalers and choosers. The model predicts that efficiency (total payoff) is maximized when signaling costs are moderate and the number of repeated interactions is high. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning, and is based on dyadic rather than group interactions, but the findings are relevant for understanding how reputation mechanisms can support efficiency in social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that mechanisms allowing individuals to signal cooperative intent and choose partners based on such signals can increase efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. While not a public goods game per se, the model is closely related and suggests that, in environments where reputation-building is possible and partner choice is allowed, efficiency will be higher than in environments without these mechanisms. The absence of punishment in the model means it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does highlight the importance of costly signaling and partner choice as alternative or complementary mechanisms. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling reputation-building and partner choice (analogous to some forms of communication or signaling in PGGs) can increase efficiency, especially when the cost of signaling is not prohibitive and the benefits of cooperation are high.

  findings: The study finds that allowing for fraternal (other-regarding) player types in spatial social dilemmas leads to the coexistence of egoist and fraternal strategies, often in spatially ordered patterns. The presence of fraternal players reduces the parameter region where only defectors survive, thus supporting higher levels of cooperation and higher average payoffs. The model demonstrates that spatial structure and personality diversity can promote cooperation and mitigate the tragedy of the commons, even without explicit punishment or reward mechanisms.

However, the model is not a public goods game and does not include punishment or reward treatments. The findings suggest that, in environments where player types or preferences can vary, the introduction of pro-social types can have a positive effect on efficiency, analogous to the effect of some institutional interventions. For direct prediction of punishment effects in PGGs, this paper is only weakly informative, but it supports the general principle that mechanisms reducing pure self-interest can improve efficiency.

  findings: The paper develops a formal model in which cooperation is a signal of time preferences in a repeated trust game. Individuals with lower discount rates (more future-oriented) are more likely to cooperate, and populations with lower average discount rates can sustain cooperation in a wider range of parameter settings (e.g., lower cost-benefit ratios, less reliable observation). The model provides payoff-based conditions for the evolutionary stability of cooperation and explains variability in cooperation and trust as a function of time preferences. No punishment, sanctioning, or public goods game is modeled. The main outcome is the equilibrium pay-off for different strategies and the conditions under which cooperation is stable.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2007). Direct reciprocity on graphs. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of cooperation via direct reciprocity is affected by the structure of the population (graph degree k) and the update rule. For birth-death and pairwise comparison updating, structured populations make it harder for cooperation to be evolutionarily stable, but easier for cooperators to be advantageous. For death-birth and imitation updating, structured populations always facilitate cooperation. The critical benefit-to-cost ratio for cooperation to be favored depends on the probability of repeated interaction (w) and the number of neighbors (k). Errors in strategy execution, which increase with the number of neighbors, make cooperation harder to sustain unless the network is sparse (small k). The paper does not consider punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. However, it provides theoretical results on how network structure and repeated interaction affect the evolution of cooperation and group payoffs in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games. The absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms means it cannot inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. Its results may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in repeated dyadic games on networks, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Kurokawa, S. (2016). Imperfect information facilitates the evolution of reciprocity. *Mathematical Biosciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of imperfect information on the evolution of reciprocity (and thus on group efficiency/payoff) depends on the structure of payoffs. When payoffs are linear, imperfect information always makes reciprocity (cooperation) less likely to evolve, reducing group efficiency. However, when payoffs are nonlinear (synergetic effects), imperfect information can sometimes make reciprocity more likely to evolve, increasing group efficiency. The analysis is theoretical, based on evolutionary stability conditions for tit-for-tat (TFT) versus always-defect (ALLD) strategies in the iterated prisoner's dilemma with mistakes and imperfect information. The results do not generalize directly to public goods games or to settings with punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how information structure and payoff nonlinearity can affect the evolution of cooperation and group efficiency in repeated dyadic games. However, it does not address public goods games, punishment, or sanctions, and thus is only indirectly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The main lesson is that the effect of information and error structure on efficiency can be non-monotonic and depend on payoff nonlinearity, but this does not directly inform the effect of punishment in multi-player public goods games.

- source: Hilbe, C. (2011). Local Replicator Dynamics: A Simple Link Between Deterministic and Stochastic Models of Evolutionary Game Theory. *Bulletin of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals interact and update strategies in finite groups (local replicator dynamics), the evolutionary outcome can be captured by a replicator equation with a modified payoff matrix. This approach allows the use of deterministic replicator dynamics to predict outcomes in finite populations, including the emergence of cooperation, risk aversion, and even the dominance of strategies that are not optimal in the classical sense. The model shows that group size has a significant effect: smaller groups can lead to the spread of spiteful or dominated strategies, while larger groups approximate the classical infinite-population results. The paper does not address punishment, sanctions, or public goods games directly, and thus does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It is relevant for understanding how group size and local updating affect efficiency and the evolution of cooperation in general evolutionary games, but it does not include punishment mechanisms or analyze public goods games with or without punishment. Therefore, it should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects. However, its theoretical insights on group size and local updating may be contextually informative for understanding the broader dynamics of efficiency in group-structured games.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2006). Evolutionary games on cycles. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary games on cycles, the update rule strongly affects whether cooperation can evolve. For the Prisoner's Dilemma, birth-death updating never favors cooperation, but death-birth and imitation updating can favor cooperation if the benefit-to-cost ratio exceeds a threshold (b/c > 2 for death-birth, b/c > 4 for imitation, for large N). In coordination games, death-birth and imitation updating can favor Pareto efficient outcomes over risk-dominant ones, unlike birth-death updating. The results are analytic and apply to two-strategy games on cycles, not to public goods games or punishment. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes as in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding how spatial structure and update rules affect the evolution of cooperation in two-strategy games (such as the Prisoner's Dilemma), and discusses payoff-based concepts like risk dominance and Pareto efficiency in coordination games. However, it does not analyze punishment, sanctions, or public goods games, nor does it report group efficiency or welfare outcomes. Its findings may be indirectly informative for understanding how local interactions and update mechanisms can support cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects in public goods games.

- source: Tan, S. L. (2019). Proximity inheritance explains the evolution of cooperation under natural selection and mutation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that proximity inheritance in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma model leads to the emergence and maintenance of high levels of cooperation, even in the presence of mutation. Cooperation is favored when offspring are placed within the neighborhood of their parents, and the effect is robust to variations in temptation of defection, selection intensity, and mutation rate. The model demonstrates that dynamic clustering of cooperators can arise, and that cooperation collapses if proximity inheritance is removed (i.e., if offspring are placed randomly or at the site of the dead individual). However, the paper does not analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes, and the game structure is a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma rather than a public goods game. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding how spatial structure and inheritance of proximity can promote cooperation, but they do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to opt out of the game (become loners) relaxes the social dilemma and can promote the abundance of cooperation, especially as the number of loner strategies increases. The effect of optionality and spatial structure (cycles vs. complete graphs) is analyzed, with the main result being that more loner strategies (or higher mutational bias toward loners) make cooperation more likely to be more abundant than defection. There is no evidence of synergy between optionality and spatial structure. The main dependent variable is the abundance (frequency) of each strategy, not group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not include punishment or sanctions as a mechanism, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its results are only adjacent to the prediction task, as they concern the effect of optional participation (loner strategies) and spatial structure on the abundance of cooperation, not on efficiency or payoff. It should not be used as direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Fletcher, J. A., & Zwick, M. (2004). Strong altruism can evolve in randomly formed groups. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper demonstrates that, contrary to previous consensus, strong altruism can evolve in randomly formed groups if groups persist for multiple generations. This is due to increased positive assortment (measured by the regression coefficient between actors and recipients) that arises after one or more generations within groups, even without kin selection, homogeneous groups, or population growth. The models show that smaller group size, higher benefit-to-cost ratio, and intermediate numbers of generations within groups favor the evolution of altruism. Stochasticity in group formation and population regulation can further affect the likelihood of altruism evolving. However, the models do not include punishment, reward, or explicit payoff/efficiency outcomes, and thus do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its relevance is limited to providing theoretical context about the evolution of cooperation/altruism in group-structured populations, especially regarding the effects of group persistence, assortment, and stochasticity. It does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency.

  findings: The paper proposes that norm psychology (the internalization of social norms) and social identity theory (the internalization of group identity) are deeply intertwined processes that together shape human norm-following behaviour. It suggests that norm adherence is flexible and context-dependent, influenced by both personal commitment to norms and the desire to maintain social identity. Punishment of norm violators is discussed as a social process (e.g., gossip, humour) that reinforces group identity and norm compliance. However, the paper does not address efficiency, payoff, or public goods game outcomes, nor does it provide empirical evidence on the effects of punishment or sanctions in economic games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not discuss game design dimensions, control or treatment efficiency, or economic outcomes. Its relevance is limited to high-level theoretical framing about the psychological and social mechanisms underlying norm enforcement and group behaviour. It may inform background understanding of why punishment and norm enforcement are important in human groups, but it does not provide actionable evidence for the downstream prediction task.
